# A.2. Program Executive Summary (max 300 words)

## The Universal Green Energy Access Program

### Objectives

The overall program development objective is to contribute to universal access to electricity in Sub-Saharan Africa by scaling up investments in renewable energy from local financial markets and the international private sector.

Specific objectives are:

- reduce the emission of CO2 through increased access to clean electrical energy for predominantly rural population in the Target Region of UGEAP (as defined below);
- reduce the cost of electrification, including off-grid renewable energy production (on or off-grid) with renewables, supplying clean energy for expanding energy demand and/or contributing to the stabilization of the national grid with additional generation;
- work with and through local financial institutions in an innovative structure to enable local banks to provide long term loans in local currency or USD for businesses that provide clean electricity solutions;
- as a result, UGEAP aims to mobilize at least 1 billion USD amount of public capital through private investment by at least 2 years, thereby significantly increasing impact.

### Development Benefits

Based on the analysis of a pipeline of transactions, through its investment activity - which will be governed by specific policies on social, environmental as well as gender aspects – UGEAP will achieve the following developmental benefits in line with the Green Climate Fund:

- Emission reduction through avoidance and reduction of CO2
- Increased low-emission energy access for the rural population, including women
- Contribution to poverty reduction
- Improvement in health care treatment
- Improvement in health conditions

### Roll-Out Forecast

UGEAP will be rolled out in 2 phases that are different in terms of the regional investment activity.

It is envisaged that the GCF for the first phase commits to contributing up to USD8 of B-Capital while for the first closing round, USD1 will be drawn and the balance would be drawn as soon as USD35 have been invested or committed to investees.

A- and B-Capital are structured as shares in a collective investment undertaking / investment fund being at closed stage. The GCF is a Luxembourg-based company constituted under the laws of Luxembourg, subject to due legal consultation.

As countries will be first rolled out in five countries ("Host Countries"), for the second phase it is envisaged that the GCF will be approached again to contribute another USD520m in B-Capital to bring the program to its full scale and support a larger number of projects. The conditions for this contribution are:

- the commitment to invests of 75% of the capital raised in Phase 1;
- satisfactory performance and demonstration of demand and pipeline to expand beyond the initial priority countries.

The first five countries have been selected based on their market opportunities for green energy access solutions, an analysis of their financial sectors, the need and feedback from project developers on the likelihood that these regions provide a viable market for the GCF's investment in terms of both the number of projects identified and NDAs. For the second phase, an analysis will be performed again on the countries UGEAP will focus on.

Swedish International Development Agency (SIDA) is expected to provide a partial credit guarantee covering the capital commitment of USD80m. The opportunity for Luxembourg, however, is a potential co-funding partner in the GCF as the B-Capital (note that the GCF and DB are not expected to become a beneficiary). The guarantee is expected to have the following features:

1. The public sector contribution can consist of paid-in capital and/or guarantees
2. The public sector contribution can consist of paid-in capital and/or guarantees

### Structure of this Funding Proposal

This application presents:
- a) UGEAP as an investment concept (the “Program Level”) designed to match funding needs of the Target Investors described above with the requirement of private sector investors for a stable, low risk return;
- project types the UGEAP will target to invest debt capital into three different project categories that are referred to in this document as Category 1 Projects, Category 2 Projects and Category 3 Projects respectively:
    - the climate mitigation and co-socio-economic benefit targets of UGEAP. The analysis of these three categories, one or more of which may be combined within a single transaction, is based on the project’s financial structure and results. These sample transactions are referred to as “Project 1”, “Project 2” and “Project 3”, respectively.

#### Category 1 Projects: Solar Home Systems

- **Solar Home Systems (SHS)** are provided with an affordable payment plan via established payment means (ie, mobile money or cash) that is structured in a way that ensures the customer can continue to pay for electricity after sales services and maintenance.
- [UGEAP] will target low-income customers in SSA, including in remote areas, a clean and affordable alternative to unhealthy, environmentally harmful, and expensive fossil fuels.
- Private sector investors are targeting targets of improved electrification while in some countries of the Target Regions the market develops very quickly at this stage and capital is highly needed for expansion.

#### Category 2 Projects: Green Mini Grids

- Companies that offer project 2 type transactions install, operate, and maintain photovoltaic (PV) based mini-grids to sell energy services in rural communities.
- These projects are designed to be scalable and incremental to increasingly match generation capacity with increasing consumer demand - effectively “growing” supply with consumption – in commercially viable, efficient, and systematically scalable markets.

#### Category 3 Projects: Green industrial energy supply and selected on-grid projects

A- and B-Capital will be structured as shares in a collective investment undertaking / investment fund being a closed-end SICAV-SIF based in Luxembourg where investors contribute capital in form of shares (subject to detailed legal conditions).

## Investment Activity

UGEAP is an investment vehicle which shall invest debt through or with local financial institutions in transactions that fall into the following two categories:

### Category 1: Off-grid green electrical energy supply for households:
- **End Beneficiaries** are households in rural areas that currently have no access to electric energy.
- **Project Companies** will be responsible for building and operating an off-grid energy supply unit that consists of a clean energy production component, a battery and a control unit sized to supply a household's demand for energy.
- **Payment Mechanism** is based on the pay-as-you-go (PAYG) model.
- The success of this business is limited so far by very low default rates due to top tier customers reached so far; an expansion to customers with lower credit quality may follow and should be performed carefully.

### Category 2: Green mini-grid and electrical energy supply:
- **End Beneficiaries** are communities that have no national grid connection but are dense enough to allow for building a green mini-grid. Final off-takers are small and medium sized businesses, households or public institutions.
- Project companies may, but not all of them do, receive grant funding for fixed installation equipment (meters, poles, etc.) from the GCF.
- **Connected households sign up on a take-it-or-pay model arrangements** for a pre-agreed load. Spare capacity is available to allow for additional off-takers and to provide grid connection services in parallel to consumption and ensure grid stability.

### Category 3: Green electrical energy supply and selected on-grid projects:
- Each project company will be responsible for building and operating an off-grid unit (e.g., agro-processing, mining, cement, ports) or service (telecom) that currently operate independent diesel based power production capacity due to lack of grid connection / availability.
- Solar photovoltaic systems are a partial or replace diesel-based generation capacity with renewable energy components (predominantly photovoltaics). Experience from projects points to cost benefits that reduce operation and maintenance costs, increase reliability and improve the quality of service provided by the project company.
- Investments can be set up as corporate or project finance structures with credit-worthy industrial off-takers on one side and power purchase agreements (PPA) covering the amortization horizon or alternatively the national grid operator.
- Target capacity size starts from 10MW for PV capacity and hence private sector debt per project could start at USD 5m.

As a public-private financial partnership and investment vehicle UGEAP bundles funds capital from:
- the project owner / sponsor in form of equity contribution (in kind as well as in cash);
- local financial institutions that invest together with or in parallel to the UGEAP;
- international private sector investors.

### Capital Structure for Investment Targets

UGEAP will transact together with local financial institutions through two alternative investment strategies:

- A funding and risk participation structure;
- A syndicated loan structure.

The capital structure is described below in Section C3.

The following table uses a single investment (defined as a "Project") as an example and provides a "look through" approach to the capital structure of UGEAP, which will be used by the Project Developers and the End Beneficiaries. The table shows the situation when UGEAP has reached its targeted scale of USD500m after completion of Phase 2. The table ranks the source of capital by the risk position (1 = being the highest risk position while two positions are equal in terms of risk). The share approximation is based on a "standard" project as an example. Each single transaction is expected to differ from this "standard" case while on average the approximate share is expected to be maintained for all projects UGEAP will invest into.

| Funding and Risk Participation | Type of Capital | Source | Instrument | Currency | Share approximation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Equity | Project developer / Project owner | Ownership of borrower | Local | 33.3% |
| 2 | Debt | Local financial institution | Loan to borrower | Local / USD | 6.7% |
| 3 | Debt | Public Sector investors incl. the Green Climate Fund | B-Capital of UGEAP | USD | 20.0% |
| 4 | Debt | International private sector investors | A-Capital of UGEAP | USD | 40.0% |

Source: Deutsche Bank

The contribution of equity as capital by the GCF will address the following barriers that exist in financing investments into clean energy production for production and consumption purposes in the least developed countries which the GCF is able to support.

## Table 5: Barriers and solutions

| Barrier | Delivery of UGEAP to overcome the barrier |
| --- | --- |
| Amortization Expectation: Infrastructure investments like those to be pursued by the UGEAP require longer amortization periods than other businesses in emerging / developing economies. | Long term capital supply: UGEAP will collect its own capital from long term oriented investors (5 years and more) and will use this capital to invest into projects as it can match the maturities of the Target Investments |
| Leverage to enable sponsorship by local project developers: The cost of equity for infrastructure investments is typically not attractive enough to be financed purely by equity or by the capital market. As such debt needs to be used, with a weighted average cost of capital (WACC). As economies expand with high growth rates, borrowing costs increase. | UGEAP shall provide debt capital: Based on market conditions and its own risk position, UGEAP will collect adequate return expectations given its risk position in the underlying investments. The combination of the returns in the pipeline and the economic rationale of the investment thesis of UGEAP, the offer by UGEAP is aimed to leverage the private sector to invest into projects that otherwise would not be realized. |
| Local banks face structural and capacity limitations: Capital and risk sharing is required to enable local financial institutions to provide sufficient long term capital the market needs to meet rising demand. Such financing is often subject to maturity mismatches which are not readily available in most financial markets in Africa due to structural constraints. | UGEAP will invest in parallel or works with its own resources: The UGEAP will be able to meet demand. The UGEAP invests in parallel or works with its own resources, and the private sector investor will share the risk of the underlying investments. As such, the capital from UGEAP will provide a long term investment horizon which will shift the critical mass to the sector to increase access to finance for infrastructure projects by creating local capacity by providing long term capital and building the capacity of local banks in project finance for renewable energy and energy efficiency in rural areas |
| Private sector investors currently do not invest outside their traditional scope: Albeit the investment and funding landscape is constantly improving and is perceived to offer attractive alternative investments, private sector investors are still reluctant to invest in new markets (regionaly as well as focus on large scale projects). UGEAP's investment proposition is a new asset class for private sector investors. | Enabling private sector investor through GCF subordination to lower private sector risk: As loss mitigation and credit enhancement measures provided by the private sector investor, their credit risk would be reduced. The combination of the returns in the pipeline and the offer by UGEAP at scale. Potential returns would provide an adequate return expectation for the risks the private sector investors would bear |
| Large capital allocation focuses on large transactions: Institutional investors that are able to commit significant funding amounts (i.e. typically no lower than US$50m just for their investment). | Large capital allocation feasible: While the single investment of the UGEAP will be in the range of US$20m - 100m, it is expected that large amounts of capital can be invested by the UGEAP into supporting smaller scale projects, including for rural households. |

Source: Deutsche Bank

## Financing / Cost Information

### B.3. Fee Arrangement (if applicable)

The fee arrangement between the Fund and the accredited entity, in case it is project/program specific:

Deutsche Bank (DB) will have two main roles in the UGEAP (and can assume further functions subject to a match between DB's resources and UGEAP's offer / capabilities), notably:
- Investment manager of the UGEAP;
- Placement agent.

**Investment Manager**

The investment services provided by DB will involve investment origination, underwriting, structuring, execution, and administration services. As compensation for these services, DB will receive a market-based investment management fee. The fee will equal 0.5% p.a. on the outstanding balance of invested capital and (i) 0.5% on committed capital, each at end of each quarter.

**Placement Agent**

Placing capital with private sector investors will be compensated for by a market-based placement fee borne by the investor. The amount of this fee is market-driven and depends on the total amount placed, the amount placed with a single investor, the type of investor who has acquired and the type of capital that shall be placed. Private sector investors shall offer either Class A or Class B to capital while GCF is allowed to invest into Class B only.

The placement fee will equal 0.1% p.a. of the amount of subscribed capital for Class B to be acquired by the GCF while outstanding.

Overall, banks manage to keep NPLs within their risk taking capacity with NPL net of provisions to Capital fluctuating around the 5% range. Profitability is high with around 3% return on average assets translating into RoEs or earnings yield.

Note that NPLs are often driven by single credit exposures that have a high weight in bank's balance sheets. It will be the target of UGEAP to create portfolios with a higher, relative granularity in which a single credit event will not have such high effects.

## Trends

There are two major risks governing the sector at this stage:

1. The potential that banking sectors may overheat which would be based on a growth of lending activities above the sustainability of real economies.
2. Continued contraction in production of raw materials linked to the cooling of Asian economies, and more specifically the Chinese economy.

Over the past century, real credit to the private sector has grown fivefold—an average annual progression of 16% over 50 years—accounting for most of the credit-to-GDP ratio of the region as a whole. Progression was driven by the oil-exporting economies and "fragile" states—which do not fall into UGEAP's initial target with the exception of Nigeria.


---
**Page 21**
---

However, some factors accompanying the rapid expansion of credit are in fact reassuring. The key point is that increased banking intermediation has been accompanied by a decline in financial deepening, shown by rising per capita incomes and an increase of the share of urbanized population. Further, the credit growth in low-income countries globally has been much smaller than in middle- or high-income regions. In all income regions went up to above 30% on average. This suggests still some more headroom for credit growth.

Nevertheless, seven countries stand out where expansion of banking intermediation has been particularly (≥20% of GDP p.a.) which are Angola, DR Congo, Guinea, Guinea-Bissau, Ghana, Lesotho and the Republic of Congo. These were the countries falling into the initial scope of UGAP.

## Program Level

### African Energy Access and Demand with focus on Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA)

An estimated 621 million people in Sub-Saharan Africa lack access to electricity; power shortages reduce the region's growth by 2-4% per year. Africa's poorest households spend USD 10/Wh on lighting which is 20 times more than income earned from work. The number of people without access to electricity and clean cooking fuels will double by 2050 to over USD 2 billion. Most will still have no access to electricity and clean cooking fuels if energy access trends continue unchanged. Energy access estimates commissioned by PIDA, the Program for Infrastructure Development in Africa (PIDA), indicate that Sub-Saharan Africa's electrification rate is projected to reach only 38% of its potential in 2050, compared with a global average of 74%. According to the World Bank, electrification rates in Namibia are relatively high at 47.3%.

This suggests that with investments in projects that give access to electrical energy for the first time, there is a high probability that electrical energy is produced using renewable energy technologies. It further allows for a sensitive approach to the issue of grid extension and electrification. The data also shows that the share of population with access to electricity increases from 40% to 65%, which is in contradiction to the typical behavior for businesses that have access to electrical grids and see energy to be available in affluence.

Indicated by mining and refining accounted for 50% of electricity consumption in Sub-Saharan Africa while much of this is concentrated in South Africa, Nigeria, Ghana and Mozambique. Accurately quantifying the installed grid based power generation is not possible as either data is not available, outdated or unreliable. However, estimates quantify the available capacity at around 120 GW with a further 35 GW under construction. The share of population with access to electricity in Sub-Saharan Africa was just about 1%. Total generation is estimated at around 600 TWh while overall energy demand is estimated to increase by 4.8% per year, which is expected to be met through the expansion of existing grids and increase the share of population with access to electricity to increase to about two-thirds by 2040, as shown in the table below.

The amount of annual unpredictably based backup generation capacity is estimated to range between 130-150 GW excluding South Africa, with a majority (>80%) going to services and industry. The actual amount of electricity being produced is likely to be higher during economic prosperous times if and when businesses can absorb the energy costs. In addition, the cost of diesel fuel has been increasing in recent years. For instance, in 2015, an estimated 90,000 barrels a day of oil is used for generators producing expenses of around US$65bn annually. Given these high costs, energy demand and the economic benefit of cheaper and reliable supply more than evident.

There are two main reasons why most African countries have not been able to provide sufficient and affordable electrical energy: (i) The continent’s electricity infrastructure is underdeveloped; (ii) Most African countries do not have a sufficient amount of renewable energy resources. In addition, the continent’s electricity infrastructure is underdeveloped. This means that the supply of electricity is often unreliable or unavailable in many parts of Africa.

The lack of reliable and efficient supply of electrical energy hinders the economic and social development of the continent. In most cases, grid-based electrical supply remains the most efficient and cheapest form to provide energy to consumers. In sub-Saharan Africa with substantial population density, there are several countries that have adopted a minimum population density threshold for specific minimum population density. This is for instance part of the explanation for the success of mobile telecommunication in these countries.

The lack of reliable and efficient supply of electrical energy also hinders economic development. The continent’s electricity infrastructure is underdeveloped. # DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 165

Africa Clean Energy Corridor (ACEC) seeks to promote a regional approach to developing a greater share of clean, indigenous, cost-effective renewable power in the energy mix. This would support African economic development.

PIDA, an example based on present and projected electricity demand statistics that assume a 6% average annual economic growth rate for Africa for 2010-40, it is estimated that this translates into electricity energy consumption growth of 5.7% per year. Over the period to 2040, 950 million people are projected to gain access to electricity in sub-Saharan Africa (SSA). The ACEC will provide a platform for the development and deployment of clean, indigenous solutions, increasingly powered by renewable energies, will play a much larger role. Against a backdrop of strong economic growth, the ACEC will be able to make significant contributions towards SSA’s energy security and access to energy by 2015 which still leaves 530 million people in the region, primarily in rural communities, without electricity.

DB estimates that around USD 20 billion was invested in renewable energy in the region between 2006 and 2014 (corresponding to roughly 1,800 MW of renewable energy installed capacity). Development finance institutions (DFIs), commercial banks and private investors are increasingly investing in renewable energy projects. However, longer needs to build-up to a crucial role in the near future, growing the market significantly, up to USD 7 billion by 2016.

## Positioning of UGEP within the social and economic context

Against this current situation, the UGEP offers an instrument that aims to:
- Enable access to electrical energy; and
- Increase the share of renewable energy production in the national energy mix to meet the existing and growing energy demand.

Research unsurprisingly shows that the capital expenditure required to meet the forecasted electricity demand and the portion of this from renewable electrical energies cannot be met with national funding sources by most of the SSA countries. Further, it becomes clear that the priority is to produce energy – which includes only a minor focus on shifting of the energy mix.

## Conclusion for UGEP targeting the SSA region

Based on our research, UGEP fits well into the regional and national strategies, given its focus on the replacement of fossil fuels as energy carrier as well as targeting energy access programs while focusing on Sub-Saharan Africa. In order to increase the likelihood for success, UGEP will first focus on a selected number of countries within SSA. The rationale is that if successful in these investments in these countries, the regional focus of UGEP to invest will be expanded to countries selected based on:
- Demand;
- Regulatory feasibility; and
- Technical and business feasibility.

## Regional Focus Phase 1

To underpin the choice of countries for Phase I beyond the actual pipeline and active demand by projects for debt funding, a number of criteria in Sub-Saharan Africa (including South Africa and countries recommended to be financed by OPEC) and their economic as well as energy and climate related indicators has been performed. For this, the ratio of people without electricity access has been put into correlation to the ease of doing business ranking by the World Bank. The results show that countries with a high ratio of people without electricity access are more conducive to the starting and operation of a local firm. Countries with a good investment case for UGEP can be found

1) IRENA 2015
2) IEA 2014
3) 2014 Sub-Saharan Africa Market Outlook, Bloomberg New Energy Finance, London, July 2014.

#### Regional Focus Phase 2
After the first phase, UGEP will start Phase 2 of the program by widening its geographical scope to new countries. To identify new countries a new analysis will be performed. Based on lessons learned from Phase 1 and an operating track record in East Africa, it is expected that UGEP will be able to broaden its activity successfully into other markets that will be chosen and analyzed carefully.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 25 OF 165

- Off-grid solar electrification of households creates electricity access for households, micro-enterprises, and their communities, and thus enables socio-economic development and growth, substituting or complementing other energy sources for low-income Sub-Saharan African households (like for instance charcoal for cooking stoves).
- Will such projects part of a nationally organized strategy / policy?
- Such type of projects will be fully private sector projects that will contribute towards reaching the Target Region Household electrification.
- Who are the end beneficiaries of the project outcome?
  - Current focus: Sub-Saharan African rural households with relatively high energy expenses and no access to grid electricity, who rely on expensive diesel or kerosene supply sources.
  - Off-grid population in Sub-Saharan Africa forecasted to grow from 600 million people in 2011 to 698 million in 2030.
  - The majority of UGAFED’s Phase 1 Target Region households are off-grid as by today:
    - First projects have started so far to electrify households but only on a small-scale compared to the potential market size.
- To ensure market acceptance different solar home system sizes are available to satisfy demand from different households:
  * Small size Solar Home Systems (SHS) (e.g. 80Wp) to fulfill the basic energy needs of those living close to the poverty line
  * Medium size SHS (e.g. 120 Wp) for the “middle income” segment of the poor population wanting to power some basic additional appliances, like charging mobile phones or using a TV set.
  * Large size SHS (e.g. 300Wp) for the upper middle class and upper class segment (most of the lowest quintile of the population, wishing to power heavier appliances such as a refrigerator).
  * Larger SHS (e.g. 500-1200 Wp) for micro-enterprises (MSMEs), enabling households to strengthen their business activities or even become entrepreneurs.
  * SHS can grow with demand, upgrading from a smaller to a larger SHS, following social, economic and environmental needs.

## What are the benefits of final beneficiaries that are getting served?

- Electricity for lighting:
- Electricity for phone charging:
- Electricity for smaller appliances, including TVs and fans:
- Electricity for larger appliances, like refrigerators or MSME machines:

- Households would otherwise use kerosene or candles for lighting, or pay a local entrepreneur to charge their phone.
- These costs can conservatively already range from USD 6-10/month without the possibility of using TVs, fans, or other appliances.

### Category 2 Projects - Green Mini Grids

Green mini grids solutions support the proposal in the following way:

- Sub-Saharan African Governments are working on several electrification projects in rural areas to increase generation capacities, still supplying to only a minority of the national demand, mainly in urban areas.

Which economic sector do the projects serve?

- Such projects will contribute towards the implementation of remote off-grid villages in sub-sahara Africa.
- It implements off-grid solar power infrastructure in the form of mini-grids for households, micro-enterprises, and their communities. This will support rural livelihoods development and growth, substituting or complementing other energy sources for low-income sub-Saharan African households (like for instance charcoal for cooking stoves).

What sort of projects part of a nationally organized strategy / policy?

- Such type of projects will be fully private sector projects that will contribute towards reaching the Target Region's electricity access target.
- Substitutes or complements other energy sources in line with the Target Region’s policy strategies.

Who are the end beneficiaries of the project’s outcome?

- Off-grid households (especially rural) with relatively high energy expenses and no expected access to grid infrastructure within 5 years, and who have a daily income below USD 2.
- Basically, similar beneficiaries as off-grid solar home supply companies while mini-grid suppliers have a stronger focus on Bottom of the Pyramid (BoP) households with a daily income below USD 2.

What are the final beneficiaries that are getting served?

- Electricity for lighting;
- Electricity for phone charging;
- Electricity for small appliances including TVs and fans;
- Electricity for micro-enterprise applications, such as small fridges, sewing machines, and agro-processing;
- Estimated electricity consumption of a typical rural household in Africa: far below 1 kWh per day as a baseline but up to 5 times higher than this.

What are the alternatives for final beneficiaries if such projects would neither exist or not be preferred by them?

As regards households: see Category 1 Projects.
- Micro-enterprises would otherwise use a diesel generator to provide electricity to power small appliances. This is no longer possible as the fuel used in these generators must be stored indoors at night for security (but not outdoors during the day), and noisy for the community. Diesel is not always available and there is high price volatility. Fuel and maintenance costs can conservatively range from USD 30-40/month.

  - Will be small projects part of a national energy access strategy / policy?

    * However, serving rural areas with alternative energy sources is in line with the Target Region's policy aims.
  - Providing reliable electricity supply to industries contributes towards achieving strong and competitive economies.

### Who are the end beneficiaries of the project’s outcome?

- Off-grid SME industry operators in agro-processing, mining, wood processing and other sectors; even suitable for small-scale farmers who can use solar power (lowering the cost of electricity generation frees up resources that could be allocated towards increasing employment).
- Off-grid commercial and industrial projects as well as micro-enterprises that lack any form of electricity supply or depend on a private run diesel generator.
  - SHS providers redeploys redundancy mitigates taker default risk as solar farms can be redeployed in case of end-of-life.

### What are the needs of final beneficiaries that are getting served?

- In remote community customer segment: Such projects improve energy access by renting solar farms (with generation capacity up to 10kW) to rural communities who in turn provide first-time energy access to grid-connected communities, through hybrid mini-grids.
  - Reduce fuel costs and increase income for small-scale leaders;
  - Reduce price volatility and more robust/redundant energy supply chain will be also greatly valued benefits;
  - Partly replace unreliable, expensive and unhygienic fossil-based fuel power;
- Reduces the need to purchase diesel generators and reduces fuel consumption of the generator (for every MWh of diesel replaced, rental solar farms add to the reduction of carbon emissions);
- Avoid upfront capital investment or long-term fuel obligations.

### Can any other type of project, such as a mini-grid, be funded either not or would not be preferred by them?

- Basically, SMEs face a poor electricity supply structure: The Target Region cannot keep up with growing demand in power supply, a large base of non-environmentally friendly installed diesel generators are being used as day-to-day or emergency solutions instead.
  - Non-renewable energy sources are often more cost-effective and less regulatory. The Target Region's electricity grids experience frequent outages; alternative diesel power is expensive (although prices went down);
- Solar-diesel hybrid farms: Provide a reliable, low-cost solution for the region;
- Rental solar farms: Offer a WtH-cost below current costs while the solar farms integrate with existing diesel generators to provide hybrid power, generating significant cost savings and CO2 mitigation.
  - Reduce fuel consumption of diesel generators and increase energy security; also provides operational flexibility;
- Redeplorable rental solar farms: Allow providing rural SMEs and residential users at attractive price levels, with annualized payback periods that are shorter than achieving maximum CO2 mitigation.

### Program Level

Sub-Saharan Africa accounts for a relatively small portion of CO2 emissions globally (0.6 gigatons (Gt) which is expected by the end of this century). However, the continent is particularly vulnerable to climate change and its impact on Africa is expected to be pronounced. Increase in temperatures is estimated to be higher for Africa compared to other regions globally. The continent is already subject to weather extremes that will increase in weight and frequency with climate change. This includes more frequent and intense droughts, heat waves, floods where sea levels would actually rise, the coastal areas, that host large parts of population and where the majority of economic activities are located.

The expansion of electrical energy production heavily depends on hydroelectric installations apart from fossil fuel based energy carriers. The investments of UGEAP target mainly off-grid solutions that are either exclusively based on renewable energy sources (Category 1) or hybridize existing Diesel based generation capacity (Category 2 and 3).

### Source: IEA 2014

The UGAP is a huge and largely untapped market of industrial off-grid diesel systems and areas that have no electrical energy supply. UGAP will serve the demand with long-term debt capital that is required to match the amortization horizons of the underlying assets.

The target of UGAP is to mitigate lifetime CO2e emissions by more than 50M tCO2e (see below in section E for further details). The impact of the program by investing in renewable energy generation capacity and measured by CO2e emissions saved and delivering towards the target to increase the share of renewable energies in the energy mix of African economies.

## Category 1 Projects - Solar Home Systems

### Project baselines are outlined as follows:

- **Sub-Saharan Africa**: Only a fraction of the total electrification reaches rural areas, while the vast majority of the total population lives in these regions;
- **Based on market information households consume 0.2 liters of kerosene per day, yielding annual CO2e emissions of approximately 15 kg per household**.
- In the Target Regions rural households spend approx. 10–25% of income (USD 15/month) on energy; even though this is a small share, it represents a significant portion of their total expenditure;
- **Off-grid solar home system (SHS) users pay an average of USD 9–12/Wp equivalent to USD 21 per month**, while after three years the SHS is paid off.

### Rural households which depend on agriculture are highly affected by climate change;

### Moreover: lack of electricity hinders children to study after dark, having negative impact on their education and consequently on their future earning potential;

### Key barrier to overcome the barrier: lack of technical understanding of and experience in last mile electrification distribution at the level of various stakeholders, e.g. policy makers;
- Market research is required.

#### Projects outcome/impact:

- SHS providers equip rural households that have different needs and willingness-to-pay (WTP) with suitable SHSs to meet their size.
- Many of them (up to 40%) “utilize the SHS for income generation”;
- In addition, SHS providers can provide LED bulbs or incandescent bulbs from every household it equips with ensuring benefits for the end-users; hence:
- SHS providers approach includes electrification and MDMs.
- SHS providers are moving away from the paradigm shift to low-carbon technologies;
- SHS providers major aim: provide reliable, sustainable and affordable solar energy technology to rural households.

A SHS provider’s offering has to be enabled by the following innovations:

- A comprehensive technological package including the photovoltaic panel, the battery, the meter, the stand-alone inverter, the control box, the compact electric appliances, the DC electric appliances themselves;
- A comprehensive customer service with affordable monthly installments and a redeemable warranty after sales service which can optionally be extended.

### Mini-grid solution (MGS) providers baseline is outlined as follows:

- **Off-grid households:** consume 0.2 liters of kerosene per day, yielding annual CO2e emissions of 0.2 T CO2e per household.
- **Micro-enterprises:** consume 2 liters of diesel per day, yielding annual CO2e emissions of 1.44T CO2e per micro-enterprise.
- **Main barrier:** no readily available, accessible, and affordable low carbon energy alternatives to traditional fuels.

### Project description:

The project displaces the use of fossil fuels through the provision of PV-based electricity services; MGS providers' approach includes electrification of MSMEs. In addition, the project aims to mitigate CO2e emission from fossil fuel from every household and MSME it connects to its mini-grid.
- Climate vulnerability is greatly reduced as access to electricity increases. Access to modern electricity services improves food security, enhances agricultural productivity, climate-resilience (such as weather reports and improved agricultural practices), and communications.

### MGS providers' offering requires the following innovations:

- A robust business model that can be scaled up and replicated in other rural sub-Saharan African conditions with remote monitoring and metering.
- A comprehensive back-end service at affordable rates and a contract lifetime service.
- Remote monitoring and metering.

## Category 3 Projects - Green Industrial Energy Supply/ Selected On-Grid Projects

### Rental solar farm solutions (RSFS) providers' baseline are outlined as follows:

- An RSFS provider is either an off-grid producer of electricity with on-site diesel generator; e.g. communities or industry sites (e.g., mines, timber sawmills, quarries and others in the extractive or manufacturing sectors).
- Or a grid-connected producer of electricity with on-site diesel generator as back-up system during power shortages (e.g. communities or industry sites).

### Three major impediments to scaling up of solar power in the African SME segment:

1. Solar up-front costs are too high.
2. Pre-financed "energy-saves" offers, based on power-purchase-agreements, require long 5-20 year life of the agreement and a large upfront investment; do not qualify investment-grade entities and almost never have a 20-year horizon decision.
3. Affordability of solar farms is low. The cost of solar farms are difficult to purchase due to large project-level costs (permitting, engineering, financing) involved.

### A RSFS providers' project outcome/impact:

- RSFS providers aim to overcome the following barriers: offering SMEs a standard, modular, and pre-deployable solar farm on a flexible, pre-financed rental basis; hence enabling customers, solid, but not investment-grade businesses, to benefit from solar cost savings without upfront investment and without the need for long term off-site financing.

# Program Level

**UGEAP’s objective is to contribute to universal access to electricity in Sub-Saharan Africa by scaling up investments in renewable energy from local financial markets and the international private sector.**

For these purposes, UGEAP mobilizes capital for local financial institutions to the benefit of End Beneficiaries in the Target Region. The different types of End Beneficiaries that UGEAP serves have different financial ability and need profiles. Dfi has developed two instruments UGEAP can use in parallel to serve the different needs:

- A Funding and Risk Participation Agreement structure (FRPA); and
- Syndication/Lease (SL) structures.

The structuring is based on following two financial elements local financial institutions require in order to serve the sector on a larger scale than current status quo allows:
- Long term debt
- Risk participation

Target Investments within the three project Categories, however, require different approaches how the long term debt and the risk taking capacity is made available to the End Beneficiaries. This is as both “assets” need to come in the same time frame.

The differences are shown in the next table, while UGEAP will pursue two solutions: FRPA and the SL structures.


| Investment Category 1 &2 (i.e. Project 1 and Project 2) | Investment Category 3 (i.e. Project 3) |
|---|---|
| Solve capital supply; long term capital to be provided to local FIs. | Strengthen the access for Local FIs outside their own balance sheet by providing capital along with local FI. |
| Risk take-over to support local financial institutions to serve regions, businesses and sectors if it is not far does come. | Risk participation in parallel with local FI. |
| Funding and Risk Participation Agreement Structure | Syndication Structure |
| Second Tier Banks |  |

The need for the different approaches are caused by the nature of the underlying businesses that are described further in the following two sections.

## Ad 1) Funding and Risk Participation Structure:

This instrument has been developed to deliver toward the End Beneficiaries of Category 1 and 2 investments and serve the following purposes:
- End beneficiaries are local households as well as micro, small and medium sized businesses (MSMEs) that are in need for light, heat, cooling (hedges) as well as electrical energy for mobile phones, IT equipment or other appliances (e.g. sewing machines).
- Households either
  - Purchase energy kits (i.e. solar home systems with a typical capacity of 80 – 200Watts) that deliver electrical energy to the household and are paid off in monthly instalments over a lifetime of up to 3-5 years after which the energy kit is legally owned by the household; or

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 33 OF 165

### (i) are connected to mini-grids after a connection fee is paid or a remuneration linked to the amount of energy being consumed (which may have a minimum off-take requirement or pre-payments).

In both cases, End Beneficiaries replace current energy carriers (kerosene, petroleum, diesel, bio-fuels) with electrical energy that is produced through their own kit or with energy generators attached to the local mini-grid. The electricity generated by these kits and generators is sold to households and MSMEs.

#### Households and MSMEs generate their income in local currency. A share of the income is used to settle lease payments for the equipment.

As a result of the business described, leasing companies create highly granular portfolios of local currency denominated claims that require debt funding; mini-grid operators create the same as a function of the amount of households and MSMEs served.

#### International investors cannot source local currency (LC) funding, a local financial institution has to be used to set up LC accounts for these claims. The LC accounts can be used in the international capital markets as well as USD based business (i.e., international trade finance transactions). Hence, local banks can play the traditional role of a financial intermediary transferring the USD capital invested by UGAF into local currency claims held by leasing companies and/or mini-grid operators.

The following chart depicts again the cash flows of the business and how a local bank can be added to provide funding.

Source: Deutsche Bank

In order to allow local banks to extend debt financing to the ESCOs, leasing companies and mini-grid operators to refinance their claims in the renewable energy generation capacity, UGAF will offer a Funding and Risk Participation Agreement that will deliver:

- long term USD denominated funding to the local bank,
- a balance sheet risk participation by UGAF of 50% generated out of the portfolio of eligible loans towards Category 1 & 2 investments.

Part of the structure is that the local bank takes the FX risk and uses its balance sheet as a natural hedge between the USD denominated funding from UGAF and the local currency denominated lending it grants to local borrowers. This is following its standard function as financial intermediary.


The following gives a description of the functioning using a simplified form of the structure while the exact arrangement will be calibrated towards the risk taking capacity of (a) the local bank and (b) UGAF. The FTPA will have two legs combined in one agreement:

- **Funding Leg**
- **Risk Participation Leg**

### Funding Leg

Under the Funding Leg, the local bank will receive a loan that can have the following exemplary features while the exact terms will be calibrated towards the risk taking capacity of the local bank along with the amount of risks that is to be transferred by UGAF:

- Loan Amount: 25m

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 165 OF 165

- Currency: USD
- Tenor: 10 years
- Interest rate: 3m US LIBOR + Margin (which will vary according to the risk profile of the bank)
- Repayment amount or amortizing subject to the risk of the financial institution and its funding profile.
- Repayment Amount: Loan Amount reduced by the Risk Participation Amount

## The Risk Participation Leg

The Risk Participation Leg details under which conditions the Loan Amount repayable by the local bank might be reduced along the following principles:

* This principle applies only if built up within the ramp-up period of [2 years] (the "Ramp-Up Period") a portfolio of loans towards Target Investments. Every quarter the local bank can report to UGEAP the loan it wishes to add to its Loss Ledger Account.
* If, at any time during the Ramp-Up Period, the Reference Portfolio has reached a balance equal to or greater than the credit risk on (2nd loss position) and that meet certain pre-defined eligibility criteria ("the Eligible Loans"), the daily stock of loans in the Loss Ledger Account will be reduced by the amount of the loan written off.
* If, after the Ramp-Up Period, the Reference Portfolio has reached a balance equal to or greater than the Loan Amount, the daily stock of loans in the Loss Ledger Account will be reduced by the amount of the loan written off. The loan from the Reference Portfolio in a Loss Ledger Account. The USD equivalent of write-offs will be the lower of (a) the USD equivalent of the loan written off and (b) the USD equivalent of the loan written off at the time when the loan was written off at the end of the quarter the write-off has been booked.
* Extraordinary revenues that are received after write-off will not reduce the Loss Ledger Account balance.

At the maturity of the loan, the Risk Participation Amount will equal the higher of (i) and the sum of (a) the debit balance of the Loss Ledger Account minus (b) the product of (i) First Loss Ratio [50%] times (ii) the Loan Amount.

Note that the applicable FX rate to the losses to be recorded in the Loss Ledger Account will be capped at 1.3x the USD equivalent of the loan written off. This is to exclude the situation where the potential loss in USD terms would increase over time.
* Note that the first loss retention will be qualified subject to the risk taking capacity of the local bank and the expected performance of its portfolio. The local bank may not have a full or partial participation on the revenues of the underlying loan portfolio. The intention is to cover the first loss price such that the bank will see a return on investment in line with its risk appetite, while maintaining an appropriate level of risk taking capacity.
* This mechanism will ultimately achieve that the local bank will have to repay only a portion of the Loan Amount in case that the USD equivalent of write-offs exceed a first loss retention amount which will be calibrated towards each individual bank taking into account its financial strength and risk taking capacity.

### Quantification of First Loss Coverage

The amount of first loss retention that will be requested by UGEAP will be tailored towards:

- the operational capacity of the bank;
- the risk assessment by DB on the underlying transactions;
- the regulatory framework where the local bank is working within.

The more the local bank has significant and sufficient risk retention to align interest of UGEAP with those of the local bank.

While the above examples make use of 15% first loss, the local bank will illustrate the functioning. UGEAP expects that the local banks will be able to cover their own local risks along the principles laid out below. It should be noted that UGEAP shall act as an enabling investor. Hence the balance between prudent risk acceptance and mission oriented target achievements is assessed on a case-by-case basis and after careful analysis of the credit risks embedded in each transaction.

The assessment of the appropriate first loss retention by the local partner bank will be conducted by DB as investment manager based on its in-depth knowledge of and track record of working with local banks in Africa through the following channels:

- As part of its investment management mandate for the existing public-private partnership funds DB manages in Africa, DB has invested in over 100 banks across Africa;
- DB enters into a strategic relationship with over 250 banks in 31 African countries through its Trade Finance Financial Institutions (TFI) which has long standing relationships with all major banks throughout Africa and offers to their correspondent banks trade finance solutions such as Letters of Credit, trade-related guarantees, etc. The TFI platform will be leveraged by DB for UGEAP;
- DB has a strong track record in analyzing and rating banks and microfinance institutions, including in Africa. The bank has conducted in-depth discussions with the local partner banks for UGEAP which have already been identified and continues the analysis for additional partner banks.

To avoid any "free riding" by local banks and to ensure they apply credit prudent lending practices, UGEAP will not fund transactions where the local bank does not provide a prudent risk assessment.

The amount of regulatory capital the bank has to maintain against the loans to be extended shall not reduce as a result of the transaction. The local bank must retain at least 10% of its equity capitalization in relation to the core equity capitalization of the local FI. Generally, banking regulation requires for first loss positions that are retained by a regulated financial institution a 1:1 coverage of the first loss retention with regulatory capital. That means, if a


---
**Page 38**

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 38 OF 165

bank had to hold against a portfolio of loans a given amount of capital, after entering into the risk sharing with UGEAP the amount of equity to be maintained should not reduce.

2) If the portfolio of underlying assets will be granular, historical data may be used to assess the potential credit quality of each loan. The model can then be calibrated for historical delinquency, default as well as losses to model the lifetime credit risk of the portfolio in a downturn scenario. Stresses will depend on the expected volatility of the underlying loans.

3) If the portfolio is off granular (i.e., portfolios of up to 10 assets), the first loss will be calibrated based on the credit quality of the single largest loan and shall become part of the reference portfolio.

Based on the above, if UGEAP were to hold a portfolio of loans in Namibia, the risk loss would be different if banks are served for example in Namibia, where current regulatory capital of banks stands at 15% compared to Zambia where the capital requirement is 8%.

Adverse scenarios and stress tests will be performed on the risk management practice of the bank. Generally, the investment manager expects that the bank provides 100% of the expected loss of its impaired loans while all the third party risks are covered by UGEAP. The higher the percentage of first loss borne by the underlying loan, the greater is the link with regulatory equity generally provides a significant buffer of first loss compared to the expected losses of the underlying loans UGEAP shall share the credit risk with. In a market average standard case, the first loss buffer shall be sufficient for the investment manager to ensure that the capital adequacy ratio of the bank concerned would be reached. It should be noted, however, that risk assessments are orientated towards future developments and hence are not exhaustive.

### Revenues for UGEAP

UGEAP will have the following sources of revenues:

* 3m USD LIBOR + Margin on the Loan Amount as compensation for providing funding to the local bank.
* The local bank will be required to transfer the USD equivalent of a certain fraction of the interest collections of all underlying loans to UGEAP. This fraction is determined by the loan agreement and the loan amount. The fraction will be calculated towards the risk amount that UGEAP will take and the profitability of the underlying loans.

The amount of UGEAP's participation on the interest income of the underlying portfolio will depend on the amount of first loss the local bank takes. The higher the percentage of first loss the local bank takes, the lower the return participation from UGEAP. In determining the amount of risk that UGEAP will take and the profitability of the local bank and the country's peculiarities, the most important element for determining the interest transfer is the acceptance by UGEAP. Note that UGEAP will have to approve each single exposure on which UGEAP will participate in the risk sharing with the local bank as well as the amount of first loss of the underlying loans.

Under this structure, UGEAP will have revenues that are entirely based on the risk since for USD capital in the market and interest income is subject to fluctuations of the local currency versus USD.

## Ad 2) Syndicated Loans

The major difference between businesses that will benefit from the FRPA structure is that businesses typically falling into this category have different requirements:

* Larger amounts of long-term debt capital which single investors generally are not able to provide;
* Can and would want to take USD denominated debt on board rather than local currency as their products are often sold in a global market, hence they need to be able to access the USD capital markets.

Businesses in this sector are often active in food processing, tourism, telecommunications, construction or raw material sourcing and export, the latter being the largest industry segment African economies rely on to generate foreign exchange. The FRPA structure is particularly suited for these businesses as it allows them to access global concepts.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 165 OF 185

grid outages, or companies have no grid connection and generate all their electrical energy required by themselves. Energy production is, however, not the core expertise of these businesses and companies would appreciate to divest the energy producing elements for the following reasons:

* Only a small proportion of the assets are revenue-generating and hence finance rather than to finance the assets with scarce equity that could be used to expand the core business activity more effectively;
* Not all the assets are not the businesses’ core expense and support teams would need to be maintained to apply best practice;
* Investments in energy producing element have different amortization horizons compared to their existing business activities, which means that the company will have to finance these investments at a higher interest rate than it could otherwise.

Regional and local development banks that serve the private sector can provide debt to businesses described above. Below the larger energy consumers, industry needs larger capacity. This exceeds the funding capacity of local institutions while international development banks are oftentimes able to finance the whole debt portion alone but cannot provide the syndication option which is preferred by many project developers. The PTA Bank will lend along them under a syndicated loan structure. This means that the syndicate partners all rank equal in terms of risk and return.

Local and regional development banks such as PTA Bank are the target partner for this structure. These banks do not have deposit taking businesses and therefore rely on debt funding from local or international markets which as well – will be able to provide the syndication option. The PTA Bank is a preferred option because it has the capacity to take on larger projects in the energy sector which in turn limits the market development. Through the syndication option, which has been developed in response to requests from local and regional development banks such as PTA, the UPEAF will employ its own resources to provide the equity portion of the project finance while the debt portion is made available locally. This will enable loans to clean energy projects and companies to be made available which could not have been financed otherwise. The PTA Bank will lend on behalf of the syndicate but it will either be the local Bank or the local bank, with local leadership as the preferred option to develop the syndication capacity of local and regional banks.

# Structure of the UGEAP

The following structural diagram details the overall functioning of the UGEAP while the key pillars are:

- **UGEAP will collect funding commitments and investments in two tranches: A and B Capital**. It is targeted that a cash flow from operations will generate total capital and B capital around 13%. B capital will be invested in its return and principal structure to A capital as the available collections of cash will be distributed in accordance with a priority matrix.
- **Deutsche Bank as investment manager will originate, structure and decide on behalf of UGEAP the investments along the instruments described above.**

| | | | Energy Supply Company |

### Source: Deutsche Bank

The overall investment process of the UGEAP and the investment by public sector contributors will be conducted in accordance with the following structure:

- **The GCF will be expected to invest into the B-Capital of the UGEAP, with an initial capital contribution of USD 80 MM for Phase I and an additional capital contribution of USD 52 MM for Phase II.**
- **Deutsche Bank as investment manager and placement agent will lever up the public sector contribution with private sector investors (A-Capital investments).**
- **Deutsche Bank is its function as investment manager, to originate, structure and close loans to and along local banks and decentralized energy service companies and other financial mechanisms.**
- **Local banks will extend loans to local “Decentralized Energy Service Companies” (DESCOs) as borrowers on long-term terms with a 10-year maturity.**
- **The D(DESCOs themselves will use the funding from the local banks and the UGEAP to provide energy supply services to the final beneficiaries: households, communities, businesses or local grid operators / utility companies.**

### Guarantee

It is foreseen that private sector investors in B-Capital may benefit from a partial credit guarantee to be issued by a AAA rated sovereign entity. The guarantee shall cover principal and no returns.

**Page 41**

# Capital Tranches

The objectives of the capital structure of the UGEAP are:
- To prioritize A-Capital investors and B-Capital investors for receiving a target return on their investment and protection against market malaise which translates into a first loss position for the GCF and other B-Capital investors/guarantors on a UGEAP World with the 2nd loss being borne by the private sector investors;
- To have an upside potential in case of a UGEAP World, where A-Capital investors will be compensated for their higher risk. In case UGEAP has higher revenues than required to meet the target returns after having paid off the claims of creditors, this compensates them for their subordinated position.

The first objective is achieved by combining two elements: the first one is the combination of minimum subscription requirements and maximum subordination requirements. The second is achieved by distributing the revenues that exceed the target return based on the amount of the "Complementary Revenue Share" of each investor, Class A Capital investors receive 100% of their Complementary Revenue Share while Class B Capital investors receive a Class B Capital to 1/4+ Class A Capital.

The second Class Capital losses may be issued in subordinate tranches, bear pro rata to their respective net asset NAV (unrealized/capital losses of the Fund only if the NAV Class B Capital has been reduced to zero. The Class A-Capital return entitlements rank senior to the entitlements of the Class B Capital, but rank junior to the claims of creditors and other subordinated investors in the event of a liquidation or winding-up.

## Table 11: The characteristics of the Class A Capital

| Reference Currency | USD |
| --- | --- |
| Minimum Subscription | $1,000,000 |
| Complementary Revenue Share | 1/4 |
| Target Dividend | Investor specific target dividend (initial guidance: 3m USD LIBOR + 5.00%) |
| Complementary Dividend | If, after having paid the target return on A- and B-Capital, there is still a surplus of revenues that are not used to fill a cash reserve, the surplus will be allocated to A and B-Capital investors subject to the amount of shares of each investor. |
| Commitment Period | 1 year |
| Maturity | Between 5 and 15 years |
| Redemption | (a) upon maturity of a tranche of shares; <br>(b) upon liquidation of the Fund; <br>(c) in case of a mandatory redemption / prepayment right of the Fund; <br>(d) dissent by the respective shareholder to amendments to issue document proposed by the Board of Directors; <br>(e) other compulsory redemption caused by statutory rulings. |
| Subordination | Fully subordinated to the creditors of the UGEAP |

Source: Deutsche Bank

## Legal Arrangement with Deutsche Bank AG as investment manager

DB will be obliged to deliver the following services for the benefit of UGEAP:

- Develop and implement a comprehensive, long-term, and sustainable program;
- Develop and implement policies and procedures in line with this funding proposal;
- Establish and develop the UGEAP's visibility and activity;
- Identify and select investment opportunities that match with the Target Investments;
- Follow its internal investment decision making processes in selecting the Target Investments to be made by UGEAP;
- Ensure that UGEAP’s activities obey the general policy developed by DB to apply to Deutsche Bank programmes funded by the Green Climate Fund and outlined below:
  - Operate within the scope of a defined mandate & operational framework, and maintain capacity to conduct social and environmental review and monitoring of UGEAP's investment activities;
  - Ensure that all investments comply with the GCF’s financial and non-financial standards;
  - Execute loan agreements, promissory notes, and other such documents describing the rights and obligations of investors under the investment, to be entered into by UGEAP.
- Agree to provide UGEAP with a copy of all documents entered into by DB in line with UGEAP’s internal processes and procedures and where necessary;
- Review and monitor the performance of investments;
- Review and monitor supercede all outstanding investments;
- Ensure an appropriate asset-liability management;
- Maintain regular communication with investors, as well as the day-to-day management of the investments, including follow-up of events of default of investments;
- Undertake any other tasks as may be required by UGEAP;
- Coordinate with the other service-providers (custodian, trustee, cash manager, legal counsel, auditors), as well as with the relevant regulatory authorities;
- Provide regular and periodic reports on UGEAP’s investment portfolio and its current financial position;
- Manage UGEAP's public relations and external communications; and ensure to the extent possible GCF's visibility.

## 2.1 Expert Recommendation on Project / Program Sponsor

### Deloitte has considerable sourcing & operating experience in the host country or other developing countries.

#### Program Level
There are three relevant areas where the capabilities of DB are crucial to the success of the UGEAP:
- Investment Fund structuring and administration;
- Investor origination and placement of capital with investors;
- Investment fund management.

### Ad 1) Investment Fund Structuring and Administration:

Setting up the UGEAP as an investment fund requires experience in choosing the right structure in order to ensure that steps 2 and 3 can be delivered successfully and are not held up by inadequate structure and unworkable procedures. Otherwise, the best investment concept may fail with private sector investors due to case formal requirements of investment structures. The UGEAP will have a broad range of potential assets under management (AUM) including "alternative" assets under management, into which UGEAP would fall, including in public-private investment structures similar to UGEAP. DB has the relevant experience needed to deliver this target.

### Ad 2) Investor Origination

UGEAP will match the GCF's and other public sector capital by attracting private investors to its A-Class capital. Deutsche Bank will act on behalf of UGEAP with institutional investors, private investors as well as development banks. These investors are sourced through one of the available sales channels of Deutsche Bank.

Deutsche Bank continuously raises assets for its open-ended funds, as well as for new fund products it launches. It raises assets from a wide range of clients across these sales channels below:

- Global Client Group (GCG)
- Relationship and Investment Advisers (RIA)
- Structured Credit Trading Desk (SCTD).

Each has its own remits for UGEAP. GCG serves as a single point of access dedicated to advising larger institutional investors on the investment opportunities available in corporates, investments companies and larger family funds on their investments. These institutional investors will undertake their own detailed due diligence around potential investment opportunities; single investment size is typically between USD10m and USD100m. GCG operates over 600 advisors across Europe, Asia Pacific, the Americas and Africa. The main clients for UGEAP are likely to be new client offices, family foundations/endowments and similar investor groups. DB will only use this sales channel where it has a strong relationship with the client office or where the client is already an existing client of DB.

RIA serves as a single point of access dedicated to advising smaller institutional investors on investment opportunities available in corporates, investments companies and larger family funds. RIA operates over 100 advisors across Europe, Asia Pacific, the Americas and Africa. The main clients for UGEAP are likely to be new client offices, family foundations/endowments and similar investor groups. DB will only use this sales channel where it has a strong relationship with the client office or where the client is already an existing client of DB.

SCTD serves as a single point of access dedicated to advising smaller institutional investors on investment opportunities available in corporates, investments companies and larger family funds. SCTD operates over 100 advisors across Europe, Asia Pacific, the Americas and Africa. The main clients for UGEAP are likely to be new client offices, family foundations/endowments and similar investor groups. DB will only use this sales channel where it has a strong relationship with the client office or where the client is already an existing client of DB.

These clients tend to invest larger amounts. KCP is closely co-operating with GCG and can be a prime


---
**Page 46**
---

source of potential investors for UGEAP:

SCTD is a banking sales channel that sells off-the-shelf products to institutional clients. The bank acts as counterparty in a trade and not in an advisory or brokering role. SCTD can buy notes from funds like UGEAP and sell these onwards to other investors, who are typically banks, insurance companies, pension funds, foundations, etc., that are interested in the underlying assets. The SCTD is capable of placing larger amounts in a relative short period (from days to weeks). This channel is only available if UGEAP has notes-like security.

Within Europe, there are two other banking sales channels that are linked to renewable energy-related investments in a total capacity of 3.496 MW. End of 2015, Deutsche Asset Management had, in addition, more than EUR 7.6bn of assets under management that are selected in accordance with environmental and social criteria.

## Ad 3) Investment Origination and Execution

Investments will be sourced through the following pillars:

* Technology owning companies and equity investors that frequently have their roots in Europe, North America or Asia;
* Relationships with businesses in Sub-Saharan Africa stemming from DB’s existing asset management mandates;
* Co-operation with other financial investors that are investing in the same sector – while being focused on equity only investments.

DB’s African network of African banks, governments and corporates developed through its platform consisting of local partners and subsidiaries is a key pillar for origination. In terms of technology providers, DB has developed and maintained relationships with over 40 partner companies who are already active in the areas UGEW will invest in and/or expand their activities in Africa.

Dedicated African experience

Regarding local presence, DB employs more than 500 people directly located in Africa or with a dedicated coverage of African countries. The bank is currently active in 19 countries across Africa including Angola, Algeria, Cameroon, Chad, Egypt, Ethiopia, Gabon, Ghana, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Liberia, Morocco, Mozambique, Nigeria, South Africa, Sudan, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia. Except for countries included on sanctions lists, there are no restrictions on DB’s geographical reach in Africa.

The main hub for DB’s African investment banking activities is the office in South Africa employing approximately 180 people. There are also dedicated teams of investment bankers located in major cities across Africa including Angola, Botswana and corporate finance. DB’s Mauritius office mainly covers securities services, cash management and fund administration and employs approx. 290 people. The representative offices in Lagos and Cairo employ 6 and 12 people, respectively.

Other African countries with dedicated investment banking coverage include: Cape Town (South Africa), Cotonou (Benin), Kinshasa (Democratic Republic of Congo), Luanda (Angola), Maputo (Mozambique), Monrovia (Liberia) and Munich (Germany).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 46 OF 165

## DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

Within Deutsche Asset Management, Deutsche Bank has a dedicated Sustainable Investments team of 38 people based in Amsterdam, Frankfurt, Hong Kong, London, Luxembourg and New York with Assets under Management (AuMs) of approx. USD 1.8bn. A team of 5 investment managers located in Frankfurt – while working closely with DB’s African Investment Group (AIG) in Johannesburg – has been responsible for the development of Africa’s largest Luxembourg-based investment company funded by public and private investors (including Deutsche Bank). It provides capital to the African market through a dedicated platform called Africa’s Sustainable Investments Trust (AST), the DB platform as well as a number of financial, E&S and industry partners with local presence in Africa.

Furthermore, it funds investment in African equities (Deutsche Invest I Africa) is managed out of Frankfurt with AuMs of EUR 1.5bn and has a dedicated team of 3 people.

DB has an award-winning track record in African bonds and between 2013-2015 issued 30 bonds in Africa for governments, corporate and banks, ranking number 1 in league tables and raising over USD$6bn on it. This provides DB with strong relationships with Ministries of Finance, African corporate and banks which can be leveraged for UGEP as well.

Through its corporate and project finance track record in Africa, DB entertains close contacts with African corporates in the energy, infrastructure and manufacturing sectors. These contacts can also be leveraged to identify investments for Category 3.

Finally, DB’s trade & export finance teams (i) handle documentary credits issued by nearly 250 banks across 31 African countries and (ii) have a strong track record of financing projects in the energy sector. The UGEP investment team can leverage this banking network together with the portfolio banks of the existing Africa fund to identify Category 3 investments.

DB’s track-record in the off-grid space

DB’s Sustainable Investment’s team based in New York has managed global microfinance funds including investments in microfinance institutions in Africa since the mid-1990s. It covers all activities related to social investments, and it is now focused on the off-grid energy sector.

Within the later the team has built up substantial track-record in the very early stage of off-the-grid market by providing a loan to light design, the world’s leading manufacturer of solar lighting solutions for off-grid households (through its Corporate Finance division). The company was founded in 2013 and DB provided USD$5m in equity financing to establish the Carbon Initiative for Community Impact (CICI) – a revolving credit facility that pre-finances carbon revenues from the sale of carbon credits generated by the installation of solar lighting systems.

In addition, DB has invested in Solar Home Systems (SHS) through its microfinance fund. SHS is managed a $4 million revolving fund which was initially jointly with the Global Alliance for Clean Cookstoves that will replicate this model across Africa and Asia. The fund provides loans to SHS distributors who are typically smallholder farmers.

Furthermore, DB has direct links to solar home light distributors servicing the BOP such as Light It, Greenlight Planet and SolaNow, most of which are potential borrowers under UGEP.

DB’s microfinance fund in the off-grid space is the Essential Capital Consortium (ECC) Fund that focuses on Social Businesses established with the intent of benefiting poor communities and directly impacting on the poor through the production of goods and/or the provision of services in three sectors: Base of the Pyramid (BoP) financial services, energy and water. The ECC fund has invested in 10 companies across these sectors including Light It, Greenlight Planet, SolaNow, most of which are potential borrowers under UGEP.

The Frankfurt-based Sustainable Investment team has gained experience in the off-grid space mainly through its investment in Solar Home Systems (SHS) and its involvement with the carbon capture/offset power plant for a mining operation in South Africa that is not based on any form of public financial support. Additionally one of the investment managers has also supported a mini-grid project development bid by Sunmine and Merensu through its technical assistance facility.

UGEP’s dedicated investment management team will make use of all the channels listed above for the sourcing and

## Project Level

The selection of the appropriate investments by the investment manager will be a satisfactory review of the existing operational capacity of the investor - the relevant service providers. The following indicators suggest a positive result of this assessment in most cases:

- Several years of already existing activity
- Evidence that the service provider believes that customer satisfaction has maintained high level.
- Trial phase in the target region successfully passed as part of the market testing approach;
- Pilot projects running with sufficient run-times to ensure technical feasibility;
- Market penetration and/or customer experience to handle the products / targeted customers;
- Network or local, qualified service providers;
- Up-to-date information on the performance and monitoring of the operations of the installations;

## Program Level

In order to align interests of Deutsche Bank acting as investment manager with the interests of investors, the bank shall co-invest up to USD50m within an environment investing at no more than 4% of the total amount of capital to be raised by UGEAP (up to USD500m when DB’s commitment shall reach USD150m in total) for which it has received internal approvals. Please note that US regulation limits the amount an investment manager of an investment fund is entitled to co-invest into (Volcker regulation). The DB co-investment is expected to be equal to 1/3 of Class B and 2/3 in Class A Capital.

## Program Level

As investment vehicle, UGEAP has two markets that are relevant to it:
