  - Amount: USD Sm – USD 30m debt portion;
  - Terms: Up to 15 years;
  - Currency: Local currency or USD;
  - Target region: Any country in Africa, Asia and Latin America equivalent;
  - Collateral: Assets that are financed by the loan;
  - Small and medium enterprises (SMEs) according to objectives unbanked customers (Category 1 and 2) or companies that are pure project finance companies (Category 3).

- On the liabilities side is the market that is of capital invested into funds that invest in infrastructure assets located

### Assets

Loans that bear the characteristics above are hardly available from local financial institutions outside South Africa. Debt capital from international private sector investors is almost non-existent. The largest capital providers are development banks, which have a limited appetite for debt financing in developing countries due to competition for good and viable projects does not exist amongst lenders while:

- The market demand for capital from the private sector is substantial and underserved with the amount of capital that is made available by the local financial institutions.
- Access to finance in combination with access to electricity were the dominant factors in the identification of the ADEs identified for local businesses, the effect of which is higher for small businesses while larger companies typically manage to cope.

The market demand for capital from the private sector is substantial and underserved with the amount of capital that is made available by the local financial institutions. Access to finance in combination with access to electricity were the dominant factors in the identification of the ADEs identified for local businesses, the effect of which is higher for small businesses while larger companies typically manage to cope.

**Liabilities**

The market for investment opportunities for private sector investors along the characteristics described above is tiny. While there are numerous governmental and multilateral initiatives, most funds that also target to involve the private sector investors focus on equity stakes rather than providing long term debt given the better return structures an equity stake provides. The only exception would be the US$ 10 billion Green Climate Fund (GCF) which has been designed with low interest rates for USD and EUR funding looking for fixed income-like products with adequate returns. USEAF falls into this category, as it comes from the growing interest by institutional investors with an impact focus to increase their investments in Africa and the world.

Independent from the site-peculiarities, it is very clear that renewable technologies have become an economic viable solution for off-grid installations or also centralized grid supply in locations with good solar resources as exist across Sub-Saharan Africa.

## Review of Country Potential

### Benin

Benin has significant solar potential with solar radiation of about 5.65 kWh/m2/day. Currently, 448 kW of PV installations are operational, primarily for villages, health centres, and telecommunications, funded by the government or the donor community.

Benin's electricity is produced by CEB (Communauté Électrique du Bénin), a state-owned binational company owned by Benin and Togo and is in charge of production, distribution, and the import of electricity in both countries. The revision of the Benin-Togo Electricity Code in August 2006 ended the CEB monopoly over electricity production, thereby opening the way for private sector participation in the generation of electricity. However, with respect to renewable electric power: CEB remains the sole buyer of electricity production to date.

Renewable energy is viewed as an option for increasing energy independence and reducing reliance on expensive imported oil. The government has set a target of 20% renewable energy by 2035 to increase the electricity production and to promote a significant contribution of renewable energy to the overall energy supply of Benin. The stated objective of government policy is to cover the whole territory within the next two decades.

In order to enable consistent resources to finance rural electrification, the government has created a rural electrification fund (REF) which provides grants for rural electrification projects and also offers state grants, donor funding, grants and


---

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 56 OF 165

### Kenya

The potential for photovoltaic energy for Kenya is good with an installation of 6.30W/m²[249]. Currently, PV installations are mainly used for telecommunication, cathodic protection of pipelines, lighting and water pumping.[25] There is an abundance of solar power potential given sufficient non-cultivated land available.

### Namibia

Namibia has one of the best solar regimes in the world with an average solar irradiation of 6.33 kWh/m²/day[27]. There is sufficient non-cultivated land available, so potential competition against arable land is very unlikely and should be assessed on a case-by-case basis.

The Namibian national government supports (renewable) rural energy production primarily by means of feed in tariffs, capital subsidies, and reduction in taxes, mainly through the Ministry of Mines and Energy supported by the Renewable Energy Fund. In 2013, the Namibian government launched a National Grid Expansion Master Plan (NGEMP) to support the roll-out of renewable energy systems to public institutions, establishment of Energy Shops and credit financing for solar technologies.

Despite numerous efforts, the experience to date of RE power mini grids in Namibia is limited to government and donor-initiated pilot projects. Regional electricity distributors appear to be unwilling to take ownership of mini-grids citing lack of viability or capacity.[28]

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 57 OF 165

Although the current energy market is strongly dominated by the national energy supplier NamPower, the government's energy policy clearly seeks to stimulate the involvement of independent power producers (IPPs) in the Namibian electricity market to help overcome the forecast short-mid-term deficit. Namibia’s Vision 2030 policy document states that the country should aim for a 100% renewable energy mix by 2050, and IPPs are seen as key players in delivering priced energy, meeting industry demands, plus some export of energy*. Namibia's Electricity Act provides for private participation in the sector.

NamPower is currently in negotiations for Power Purchase Agreements and Transmission Connection Agreements with Daz Power (wind power generator) and GreenNam (solar PV). Moreover, the Ministry of Mines and Energy has implemented the Renewable Energy Procurement Mechanism that requires tendering for all renewable energy projects under 50 MW in size*.

### Nigeria

Nigeria has an enormous solar energy potential with solar radiation of about 5410 kWh/m2/day*. The government is in charge of the electricity distribution and generation in the nation under the umbrella of Power Holding Company of Nigeria (PHCN) and National Electricity Regulatory Authority. During 2012/2013 Nigeria has been privatising the state-owned Power Holding Company of Nigeria (PHCN), with the intention to increase investments and increase the total generation capacity.

While there are several grid-connected photovoltaic technologies in Nigeria that have been installed, this has led to private sector participation in the electricity generation sector in general, and a number of operational IPPs is active in the country today. Establishment of off-grid generation is encouraged by the government through a moratorium on import duties for solar energy equipment and other incentives such as tax credits and feed-in tariffs. There are several renewable energy projects, feed-in tariffs for solar energy, wind power and small-hydro. There are several on grid photovoltaic projects in the pipeline which are expected to be constructed over the next years.

### Tanzania

Tanzania has high levels of solar irradiation of 6.4 kWh/m2/day*. Solar resources are especially good in the central region of the country, and are being developed both for off-grid and grid-connected solutions. There is sufficient non-cultivated land available, so potential competition against agriculture is low and unlikely and should be assessed prior to any large-scale development.

The Tanzanian national government supports (renewable) rural energy production, primarily by means of feed-in tariffs, capital subsidies, and reduction in taxes, mainly through the Ministry of Energy and Minerals, mandated by Rural Energy Act 2013. The Tanzania Solar Energy Development Agency (TANSEA) is responsible for implementing the Renewable Energy Fund Board, and Agency, exemplified by the procurement of a USD 250 million loan to fund roads and rural electrification projects.

The contribution of the private sector is significant and encouraged. Only 59% of total capacity is supplied by TANESCO, while IPPs and Energy Power Producers (EPPs) provide 26% and 13% respectively, which they sell exclusively on the grid. Since 2009, Tanzania has been implementing the so-called Small Power Projects (SPP) program geared towards the promotion of private sector participation in the power sector, but also for renewable energy scale-up and accelerated electricity access to the Tanzanian population. The SPP program allows small renewable and co-generation facilities of up to 5 MW.

## Kenya

### Current Baseline:

At the national level, wood fuel and other biomass accounted for about 68% of the total primary energy consumption, followed by petroleum at 22% and electricity at 9%.

Biomass provides for more than 90% of rural household energy needs. The main sources of biomass for Kenya include firewood, charcoal and crop residues.

Estimated national electricity demand is 1,191 MW. Effective installed capacity under normal hydrology is 1,429 MW, leading to a reserve margin of 238 MW, below the recommended reserve margin of 30%. The climatic conditions in Kenya are such that there can be significant variations between summer and winter generation and load demand shortages which culminated into power rationing.

Conventional energy sources (coal, oil, natural gas, nuclear power) and wind are 52.1%, 17.8% and 0.4% respectively while fossil based thermal contribute 32.9%.

While geothermal and hydro also dominated new investments into Kenyan renewable energy assets until 2016, with the largest single investment in renewable energy investments since with new investments summing up to more than 1bn USD (namely Lake Turkana).

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 59 OF 165

- Government is undertaking efforts to provide lighting and water pumping PV installations to public institutions in Arid and Semi-Arid Lands where there is no access to the grid. These institutions are mainly primary and secondary schools, Dispensaries, Health Centres, Administration units and public water wells.
- According to the National Electrification Plan, 100% of Kenya’s population will have access to electricity by 2030.
- The national electrification rate is only about 22%, in rural areas lower.
- Solar PV systems are being installed on public institutions in off-grid areas. The target is for the grid to deliver 50% of energy by 2016 and 80% by 2030.
- Charcoal is the second most popular type of cooking fuel used by 13.3% of households.
- Kerosene-based lamps are the leading source of lighting for Kenyan households*. Over 79% of households use plant-based oil as a primary or secondary source of lighting about 14%, while paraffin is the most predominant in rural areas (87% of rural households).

## Market Potential

*Population density of 60 persons per km² makes it economically unviable to build a national grid that reaches less than 10% of the population.*
- Isolated grids or decentralized production facilities will have to be an inherent part of the Kenyan energy development strategy. The government is considering “by design” a greater degree of electricity supply through a distribution network all over the country, similar to the mobile telecommunication networks.
*The national electrification target of the Government of Kenya as set out in the Vision 2030 is to achieve 100% access by 2030. This will be achieved by connecting households and institutions directly to the grid or being connected to electricity.*
- At this stage, mini-grids are only playing a relatively minor role in supplying electricity to Kenyan households.
*Currently 21 mini-grid stations are in operation, another 10 are under construction. These mini-grids are mainly supplied by diesel generators and/or solar PV systems. The Kenya Power & Lighting Company Limited (KPLC), the electricity distribution company also responsible for managing the national grid. That means that electricity consumption in the existing mini-grids is heavily subsidized through the tariff structure (cross subsidies).*
- The Kenyan government has set a target of 10,500MW by 2030 to be generated from renewable energy and diesel based generation given to hybrid generation (based on Solar PV and/or wind); to improve the cost efficiency of operation and reduce carbon emissions. Government seeks to actively integrate the private sector to achieve this goal.
- The UGAEF offers the chance to challenge this forecast and contribute to further increasing the share of renewable energy in Kenya’s electricity supply.

## Retail segment (BSC)

*Less than 10% of total population has access to electricity and are not connected to the national grid. In the off-grid areas, mainly in the north and east of Kenya, KPLC and KenGen provide power by means of diesel power plants which are connected to a distribution network. However, the off-grid power plants shown in Table 7 only generate energy for 0.8% of the total electricity supply in the country. The government has announced its target for investments of Category 1 and 2 Project types with a peak demand of 9,040kW in off-grid areas which is expected to increase to over 15,000kW by 2030.*

## Namibia

### Current Baselines
- Estimated national energy demand is 1,713 mtoe;
- Biomass fuels (i.e. wood and charcoal) account for over 18% of primary energy supply; oil products 64%, hydro 7%, coal 5%;
- Total grid installed capacity 393 MW and the interconnector capacity stands at 900 MW
- Of the total electricity generation, 27% is hydropower and 37% thermal (coal, diesel & gas); share of renewable energy other than hydro is negligible.
- Fuel and mining imports account for 19% of total imports of Namibia which makes RE transaction highly beneficial to the country;
- 40% of population without access to electricity, in rural areas 75%;
- 90% of households use wood as their primary cooking fuel; 20% paraffin, 8% electricity;
- Diesel generators or solar photovoltaic home systems are alternatives not readily available for at least 95% of the rural population due to high upfront costs and supply chain challenges.

### Overview
Over the past decade Namibia has been experiencing a rapid increase in energy demand, reaching a 430MW power deficit by 2015.
The share of renewable energy seems particularly high with 71 % of the total primary energy supply (TPES) in 2009 and 68% of the electricity generated in the country. However traditional biomass accounts for 41% of the TPES.

Draft net metering rules are prepared allowing to feed-in electricity into the grid (with capacity below 500kW) which will contribute to the stabilization of the grid as well as support the decentralized expansion of capacity

| Baseline |

- In 2009, the official figures according to the Ministry of Energy and Minerals of Tanzania were:
    - Estimated national energy consumption: 22 million tons of oil equivalent;
    - Biomass fuels (i.e. wood and charcoal) account for over 50% of primary energy supply; petroleum 8%, and electricity 1%;
    - Total grid installed capacity 1,051 MWP;
    - Of the total grid capacity, 32% is hydroelectric power, 33% hydropower and 47% thermal (coal, diesel & gas); share of renewable energy other than hydro is negligible.
- Fuel imports are a major concern for Tanzania which makes renewable energy transaction highly beneficial from a macroeconomic standpoint:
    - Off-grid installed capacity 143.1 MWP; thereof 69% coal, diesel and gas, 7% hydro, the rest is biomass-based installations (e.g., small-scale biogas plants and solar home systems).
- No photovoltaics nor wind production capacity in 2009.
- Most of the population has access to electricity, but only 15% have access to clean cooking fuels (almost no biomass resources available on site).
- 86% of population without access to electricity; in rural areas: 98%;
    - In rural areas, 92% of the population uses kerosene or candles as a primary source of lighting, chosen for their availability and affordability.
- Diesel generators or solar photovoltaic home systems are alternatives not readily available for at least 95% of the population due to high upfront costs and lack of access to credit; challenges:
    - Overall expected growth in power demand: 10–15% per annum.

## Baseline - Update (2014+)
- Demand: Current annual per capita consumption below 100 kWh (as reference point, the US per capital consumption is 13,964 kWh/capita; source: Daeproj/De The Wind Flare data).

### Category 1 Projects

The off-grid energy market in Sub-Saharan Africa is comparably young. The amount of successful players is still limited in numbers—but growing at fast pace:
- Competitors are mainly foreign companies or similar companies active in developing countries: more than 50.
- There is a low concentration of competitors on a large market; aggregate market penetration below 5% in Sub-Saharan Africa, while increasing.

There is a high demand for off-grid competition as demand is significant and every business concept has its specific features:
- Three relevant segments for relevant market players: solar lanterns, pico systems (2-8 Watt), and larger, productive Solar Home Systems (compare market segmentation above).
- Competitors in the pico range (up to 10W): limited performance during rainy season, but considered to be an entry point.
- Category 1 Projects introduce an affordable up-front payment (like a “connection fee”), combined with pay-as-you-go or other flexible payment modalities.
- There are two relevant cost elements for customers: a) price per Watt peak installed, and b) payment modality:
- A typical payment profile is outlined in the table below.

## Category 2 Projects
- The main competitor to Category 2 Projects is the use of traditional fuels and "appliances". This includes the use of kerosene wick candles and paraffin, as well as battery- and diesel-based electricity for phone charging, radio, TV, etc. These are typically more expensive than solar PV systems in terms of initial cost or other unit energy costs.
- Given the price competition are new PV based products and other social enterprises in off-grid electricity.
- There is competition among suppliers in the price range of USD 10-500 range per system;
- Other popular appliance providers provide products in the USD 10-100 range using similar pay-as-you-go systems
- Category 1 Projects are also an alternative to mini-grids:
- Category 2 Projects prices shall be competitive with Category 1 Projects;
- Category 2 Projects price systems shall consist of elements similar to Project 1: (1) Solar PV; (2) Plus energy use; (3) The sales can be either metered in a traditional pay-per-use approach, or in bundles. A bundle is a package of one or more appliances with an expiration date attached. A bundle may also include an appliance on lease, for instance a TV or a stereo; in such cases, the appliance ownership is transferred to the customer after a certain number of bundled payments, with repayment in 6 to 3 months.

## Category 3 Projects
- Category 3 Projects include providers of rented photovoltaic farms which is an innovative business model.
- The business cases are built on robust yet very low price of which enables further scaling;
- Investors are active in the segment "Off grid commercial market (B2B)", although solution can also be useful for other segments such as B2C, B2G and B2B.
- The success is driven by 1) cost leadership and replicability; 2) proprietary advanced hybrid systems combining solar PV with diesel generators and battery storage;
- The offer combines the benefits of the generic rental model (fast deployment, no up-front investment, flexible duration, asset-backed finance), but with the benefits of solar power (lower price, no carbon emissions);
- This segment is also attractive for governments as it can be used to provide off-grid electricity in potential SME scale, offering financing solutions along technical ones.
- Customers are typically small businesses or individuals based on envisaged energy consumption;
- To the commercial customer, photovoltaic power is still cheaper than diesel (Provided US$/Btu price is higher than - USD 20);
- Business model is not based on subsidies and hence, governments are typically not involved, apart from business permits.

### C.E. Regulation, Taxation and Insurance (if applicable)

The project will require licenses or permits required for implementing and operating the project/program, the issuing authority, and the date of issue or expected date of issue.

## Program Level Assets

- Licenses, Approvals, other regulation
- Granting debt to regulated financial institutions in the Target Region does typically not require a special license. Registering and registering, however, sometimes apply to the import and export of international currencies like USD.
- The UGEAP will obey the respective regulation and DIB does not see obstacles in the existing rules towards the implementation of the project/program.
- Under the syndicate option, UGEAP will participate in the loan to the project company directly. Typically, this is achieved through the acquisition of claims arising under the loan agreements from the syndicate lead, which can either be a commercial bank or an international financial institution.

All countries have regulation in place that governs international currency transfers later for national statistical purposes and banking oversight. None of the 8 target countries though restrict international currency flow to the point that would materialize our investment targets as to equity.
- Tax
- Typically, withholding tax applies to the interest rate payments that are to be paid from the project company to UGEAP

## Liabilities

### Licenses & Approvals

For an investment vehicle based in Luxembourg, the local financial markets supervisor has to approve the private placement memorandum (issuer document which sets out rights of investors in this fund). Approval is once the prospectus has been approved by CSSF (Commission de Surveillance du Secteur Financier). Structuring the Fund in accordance with Luxembourg legislation will be ensured by a local and experienced law company to be hired by DB.

For investment funds that fall under the Alternative Investment Fund Markets Directive (AIFMD), the fund manager may require to be licensed within the European Union to fulfil its role as investment manager. Deutsche Bank entities hold no required licenses which would also apply to UGEAP.


### Tax

Deutsche Bank is not entitled to provide tax advice. To understand its tax position, investors should seek their own tax advice which can vary from the general situation described below.

Interest income is taxable in Luxembourg and subject to withholding obligations.

Payments of interest are free of tax while resident individuals of Luxembourg can opt for either a 10% withholding or declare interest revenues; the 10% tax is final if residents act in the context of private wealth;

Since January 1, 2015, Luxembourg officially started to provide information to other tax authorities on interest and similar income subject to up to 30% withholding tax applicable before. The Luxembourg Paying Agent is responsible for reporting for Luxembourg tax regulation.

**VAT**

Luxembourg VAT may be payable in respect of taxes invoiced for services rendered to the issuer. The payment of interest or dividends on debt instruments issues are typically not subject to Luxembourg VAT.


### Investor Level

Holders who derive income from investments made by UGEAP or who realize gains on the disposal / redemption, are subject to Luxembourgish taxation unless:

- the holder is so deemed to be resident in Luxembourg for Luxembourg tax purposes;
- such income or gain is attributable to an enterprise or part thereof which is carried on through a permanent establishment in Luxembourg.

In addition, national taxation for each investor applies.


### Insurances

It is foreseen that a partial credit guarantee will be part of the structure to benefit of private sector investors (see above in section C.3).

# Board of UGEAP

As an investment fund, UGEAP will have a Board of Directors that will be proposed by Deutsche Bank to shareholders investing in the Fund. The Board of Directors will oversee the activity of the investment manager and other service providers appointed by UGEAP for the purpose of managing a portfolio of investments on behalf of its shareholders and its interests. It is not foreseen that representatives of the CGI will receive a board seat. The fund documentation will state that the board members will need to include members proposed by CGI in accordance with Luxembourg legislation; board members represent no single shareholder but are responsible to all shareholders simultaneously.

## Investment Management

UGEAP as an investment vehicle will be managed by Deutsche Bank as Investment Manager (other Deutsche Alternative Asset Management entities such as DWS, BlackRock, and the UK-based Schroders, Real Special Invest GmbH (Germany) or another legal entity part of Deutsche Bank group). A sample list of tasks is provided in section C3 above. Under the investment advisory agreement, UGEAP assigns full authority to the investment manager to execute investments on its behalf and administer the Fund.

Certain decisions will require a positive voting from the investors in UGEAP. In line with local legislation (Luxembourg) this includes mainly changes to the prospectus, which governs the relationship between UGEAP and investors, and that will be subject to approval by the Investment Committee (IC).

The investment management team will be led by a fund manager who will have the overall responsibility of the mandate. The Fund Manager will oversee a team of investment managers that have the task to support delivery towards the Fund’s objectives. This includes the development of new investments, as well as monitor the existing portfolio of investments, a function which is supported by Investment Administration. Decisions on: acquisition of assets and policies; new investments; and amendments and interventions on existing investments that have an economic impact to the performance of the Fund.

require the approval of the "Investment Committee" (IC) which is staffed by about 3 investment specialists with knowledge and experience regarding the underlying type of investments. The IC will receive a recommendation from the Fund Manager, who represents the Investment Management team, on all major decisions for the Investment Management team. Decisions from the IC are binding for the Fund Manager who represents the investment management team in the IC.

The role of the risk manager is to review the proposal from the investment management team and provide his recommendation to the IC independently. The risk manager will participate in IC meetings.


# Guidelines and Policies

As mentioned in section C3, the Investment Manager's actions will be governed, amongst others, further by the following internally developed and maintained policies and guidelines:

- Investment Policy
- Asset-liability management policy;
- Governance of Deutsche Bank Programmes funded by the Green Climate Fund;
- Social & Environmental Guidelines;
- Operating Guidelines.

All Guidelines and Policies governing UGEAP comply with Deutsche Bank's internal policies and procedures as well as with CGI policies and procedures as outlined in B.07/02 - Annex III.

# Investment Policy

The investment policy contains a range of rulings and is prepared by the investment management team:
- **Rulings on specific issues** which include the staffing of the investment management team and the IC;
- **Detailing the investment decision making process** described above;
- **Definition of eligible investments (including definition of eligible investors)** in line with the definition on the Trust Agreement;
- Ensure that investors will fall within the capacity of DB as accredited entity of the GCF in accordance with the requirements of the Trust Agreement;
- Target region of investment activity (subject to limits);
- Definition of eligible investment instruments;
- Definition of maximum exposure to any single instrument or instruments it may contain;
- Limits on maturities and guidance on pricing structure (which is reviewed routinely);
- Limits on portfolio composition-restrictions and limits to be delayed during the lifetime of the investment activities;
- Monitoring and reporting processes, routines and content of reports to be produced for the benefit of the IC as well as national authorities.

The investment policy sets a number of limits that are designed to protect investors against a lack of granularity within the investment portfolio. Certain limits are also documented in the draft Term Sheet that would apply to the GCF investor. Additional limits apply to the following layers:

## Country Level Limits

Single country concentration limits (SCLs) are applied through its investment activity. Country specific risk is measured by credit ratings, sovereign impact rating and convertibility risks on foreign currency exchange measured by T&C risk ratings as well as systemic risks that affect the whole economy, which includes political and legal risks (measured by the credit rating of a country).

The SCLs are applied to the total exposure of disbursed and committed capital to a single country as a percentage of the cumulative amount of all investments (disbursed and committed) of UGEAP. The limit is binding 36 months after the first investment have been executed to allow the portfolio to build up while it is subject to ongoing monitoring and reporting requirements. SCLs are applied on a rolling basis, i.e., every year for each Country. Limits will apply subject to the internal grading category a country falls into. While the grade is denoted using the rating scale of international rating agencies, the internal grade would apply.

| Exposure Category | Investment Grade |
| --- | --- |
| Investment Grade | 30% |
| BBB | 20% |
| B and CCC | 15% |
| Unrated | 10% |

## Single Financial Institution Limit

UGEAP is exposed to the credit risk of banks UGEAP lends to under the FRPA structure. A limit will be set in relation to the invested capital into a single financial institution in relation to the total investment portfolio of UGEAP.

### Selection of Financial Institutions

The investment policy will further detail the selection criteria for the single transactions the UGEAP will take a risk position on as well as the local financial institutions UGEAP will either finance (FPF structure) or work jointly with (Syndicated loan structure).

Banks are chosen after a careful financial risk analysis that spans over the following areas:

- **Financial position and strength**
  - Capital adequacy
  - Governance and ownership structure
  - Quality of management
  - Risk management systems
  - Regulatory status and quality of local regulator
  - SAI Management Systems

### Financial Analysis

Deutsche Bank performs a detailed financial risk analysis that covers the following areas:

- **Growth of the bank**
- **Loan book quality / customer type / NPL provisions, collateral / related party exposure**
- **Group concentration by sector / industry concentration / regional concentration**
- **Liquidity position of the bank**
  - profitability
  - cost base and development
- **Funding basis including costs of funding and maturity structure**
- **Incorporation of any report or other connections with long-term capital providers like DFIs**
- **Equity (Ideally Basel II standards)**

### Governance & Ownership Structure

Banks are expected to adhere to international standards on AML, ATF.

Further, banks are expected to have a transparent and shareholder value-oriented governance structure. Regulatory authorities have to stay below the maximum permitted leverage ratio.

Banks with a strong ownership structure are preferred in order to allow for growth of the bank beyond the inherent profitability.

### Management Capabilities

Management capabilities will be screened across the hierarchy to assess the technical capabilities and the required experience to manage the bank on the basis of its market position and size. Banks should have a robust management information system (MIS) in place.

### Risk Controls and Procedures

On-site due diligence is performed to analyze the routines and procedures that are in place:

- S&E Management Systems: All banks UGEAP will work with are expected to have an appropriate Social and Environmental Management System in place.

## Documentation

The agreements signed by the UGEAP and (ii) banks under the Funding and Risk Participation Agreement as well as (iii) borrowers under the syndicated loans will include, based on templates that will have been developed by a reputable, internationally active legal counsel, a clear documentation of the following points:
- financial covenants;
- maturities and time lines;
- reporting requirements;
- working capital requirements which includes adherence to S&E standards; financial covenants as well as adherence to national law and international best practices.
- conditions precedent (which will include the requirement for all licenses and permits to be valid as well as a social and environmental impact assessment in case standards of UGEAP require this);
- conditions subsequent for follow-up disbursements;
- role of the UGEAP representative, including the UGEAP manager; event of defaults and mandatory prepayment clauses;
- at least one (1) project per bank will be included in the portfolio of loans that are granted using the funding facilitated by UGEAP which will match the one UGEAP is set to bind to;
- eligibility criteria that will be matched with those of the UGEAP on the selection of projects that deliver to the policy objectives:
    - CO2 emission reductions within the limits which would apply to the UGEAP;
    - gender equality;
    - additional socio-economic benefits UGEAP targets

Essentially loan documentation is a mirror of the setting of the UGEAP in terms of targets and limitations with regards to the borrower, including but not limited to the environmental and gender specific aspects to be obeyed.

In case due diligence on banks identifies weaknesses, like a still acceptable deviation from UGEAP's expectations on a working and acceptable S&E management system with the bank, the agreement may include covenants on pre-defined default events.

Non-adherence may lead to a mandatory prepayment event or an event of default.


## Asset-Liability Management Policy

UGEAP is a closed and investment vehicle with a charging asset and liability profile. The yield for investors will depend on the interest income actually received from the invested assets and expenses to various service providers of the UGEAP. It is the task of the Fund Manager to maximize the available interest income. Certain risks can be hedged and are managed through the capital structure, including by holding them or not while other risks are to be mitigated structurally through decisions on capital demand and supply.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 75 OF 165

Assets and liabilities of the UGEAP are expected to have different financial and credit terms. Hence, investors are exposed to the following risks that can negatively impact their return as well as potentially the capital repayments UGEAP would be able to make:

- **Maturity Risk:** Mismatch in the timing of payments UGEAP is entitled to receive and the timing of payments UGEAP is expected or required to make.
- **Currency Risks:** The currency in which assets are denominated may be different to that of the shares. Interest Rate Risk: The income on the assets may be different to expected coupon payments on the shares.

**Assessment:** UGEAP holds investments in terms of cash equivalents and debt towards financial institutions and / or companies within the Target Region. Loans may be provided by banks with a leverage ratio of 100% and will be denominated in USD exclusively.

Liabilities: UGEAP will issue capital in form of shares. Shares have a fixed redemption date at which UGEAP pays dividends to shareholders, and this is contingent on the performance of the investments to generate liquidity upon maturity. The return on the capital issued by UGEAP will be variable and will equal to the amount available for distribution in accordance with a Priority of Payments, after having covered senior operating expenses of the UGEAP.

A more detailed description on the management of ALM risks can be found in 2 - Annex 1 - ALM Risk Management.

## Risk Management & Workout Processes

The risk management framework of DB as investment manager of UGEAP follows a three steps approach to deal with performing assets:

- **Identification**
- **Mitigation**
- **Workout**

In the following sections these steps will be explained in more detail.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 76 OF 165

Whenever covenants breach pre-defined thresholds, whether financial or non-financial covenants, this will lead to a re-classification of the underlying asset as non-performing and provisions will be made accordingly. Another reason for a re-classification is a payment delay of more than 90 days following Base II default definitions. This non-classification and provisioning will also be communicated to the Fund during the consecutive regular quarterly reporting.

## Mitigation

Following the identification of a non-performing asset DB aims to assess the overall financial and operating risks, as well as the social and environmental implications of the project. For that is more detailed analysis of the latest financial statements, financial projects and the current operating and financial strategy are performed. Based on this assessment, the investment management team typically recommends one of the following options to the Investment Committee:

- (Temporarily) Waive the covenant breach, in particular if the breach is expected to be healed in the foreseeable future.
- Adjust the covenants, if particular if the current thresholds are not considered realistic and/or achievable any more. This could include a temporary adjustment of financial projections or adjustments observed in merger & acquisition cases which tend to have a significant impact on key financial covenants.
- Propose remedial action by the manager of the project to avoid further deterioration of the financial situation, including but not limited to: (i) changes in the business plan; (ii) changes in the assumptions of the underlying business plan, possibly including management changes; or (iii) changes in the composition of the capital structure. The Investment Committee may also require that certain conditions be met before any adjustments are made.
- Demand complete or partial capital repayment if remedial actions are considered insufficient or impossible.

Before any repayment can be effected an assessment of the potential social and environmental implications must be conducted.

## WorkOut

If UGEG demands a complete or partial repayment of capital, the likelihood of complete repayment must be assessed in accordance with para 2. Mitigation. If a complete repayment is unlikely, workout teams will be created. These teams can access dedicated experts from legal and risk departments in DB. In particular, DB’s RMA (Risk Management Advisory) team has a successful track record in workouts and can support the implementation of the workout.

In a workout scenario, the team re-negotiates the contracts; assesses the collateral values available and starts their liquidation if necessary. Given the relatively weak legal enforceability in some countries under consideration liquidation may be slow and subject to delays. This could lead to an increase in the overall recovery time delay. Furthermore litigation of collateral against our contract partner (i.e. financial institutions) could impact the ultimate recovery rate, especially when it comes across vulnerable groups. Against this background DB considers a workout process to be the ultima ratio only.

Benefiting from DB’s global network and significant-in-house Africa expertise, DB’s investment management team have a track record of finding solutions for critical exposures that ensure their going concern and avoid workouts.

## Work-Out Scenarios under the Funding and Risk Participation Structure:

Once UGEG has given consent for an exposure to become part of the Reference Portfolio, the credit risk arising from the underlying exposure is partially borne by UGEG while the servicing of the loan remains with the local bank. Once the loan becomes non-performing, the local bank will be required to provide a detailed analysis on why it became non-performing and what remedial actions have been taken. The local bank may also be required to become part of the ongoing credit surveillance process. Local banks will be obliged to manage exposures in line with their

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 77 OF 165

### Loss Allocation

In case the bank (partially) writes off claims for principal payments it has against the underlying borrowers, the following conditions need to be fulfilled for UGEAP to bear partially the losses:

- the write-off must comply with international accounting practice;
- the write-offs are limited to claims for principal repayments.

UGEAP shall have the right to verify the amount of the write-off which in practice would be performed by a local auditing company mandated by UGEAP.

### Work-Out Process

In case of a default or bankruptcy, local banks are generally best positioned to deal with the situation under local law while UGEAP shall receive a right to intervene with decisions by the local bank that, subject to a materiality clause, will require the consent of UGEAP. The following actions shall be required:

- give consent to the replacement of management or other action by management of the creditor requiring approval by the lender;
- agree to the appointment of a liquidator required under local law;
- agree to any restructuring of the cash flow profile of the loan;
- initiate the redirection of cash;
- initiate and effect the foreclosure of security; and
- agree to the sale of the exposure to an enforcement agent / non-performing assets specialized company.

In principle, any action by the local bank to give up rights (such as security or disposal of the asset) will require the consent of UGEAP. Failure to obtain such consent will cause the exposure to retroactively become ineligible to be part of the Reference Portfolio and UGEAP shall contractually have no risk exposure any more to the loan.

Operationally, three elements will be employed to reduce the probability of incurring non-performing assets:

a) Selection of the right local bank as a partner institution of UGEAP based on DB's comprehensive experience with partners in the region. The selection process will consider background in banking analysis (with the fund manager rating agency experience for financial institutions); and

b) Acceptance of exposures to be added to the Reference Portfolio to include ideally only simple funding structures;

c) A close and ongoing interaction with the lending department of the local bank as part of supervision.

### Projects

Projects should describe in detail the governance structure of the project, including but not limited to the organization structure, chief responsibilities of the project management unit, steering committees, executing entities and so on. Describe construction and supervision methodology with key contractual agreements. Describe the credit analysis of the counterparty for the project, including the completion of construction, if applicable, provide the credit analysis of key counterparties of key contractual agreements and/or structural mitigants to mitigate risk.

Details on the project level can be found in Annex C.7

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## Value Added for GCF Involvement

Please specify why the GCF involvement is critical for the project/program.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) support to UEGAP is critical for the following reasons:

- Facilitating the transition of the private sector into a new asset class
- Overcoming structural limitations of local financial markets and international investors
- Triggering the development of local capital markets with higher return profile
- No alternative source of public funding at scale is available
- Local banks are ill-equipped in terms of risk and capability building required to deal with renewable energy-related businesses
- Facilitating the transition of the private sector into a new asset class

Financing access to electricity in Africa has traditionally been a public sector mandate. In remote areas, the approach to electricity access was typically grid-based and thus not economic, requiring investment grants from the public sector to capture the benefits of electrification. However, with the advent of mini-grids, solar home systems, off-grid lighting projects, and associated cost reduction in on-grid renewable energy solutions have now created the opportunity for Africa to leapfrog into a new asset class. This new asset class is characterized by private sector investment in renewable energy, with a short operational track record, currently limited in scale and operating in an unregulated environmental regime.

The private sector, i.e., local financial institutions and international institutional investors, therefore will not provide 100% of the required capital from their own balance sheets. Projects that are categorized as category A and B projects would also remain for category C projects. A public-private partnership with the support from the GCF is therefore required to finance these projects and facilitate the transition into this new asset class.

Overcoming structural limitations of local financial markets and international investors

UEGAP will provide local currency debt finance to (DE)SOS through local financial institutions (FiIs) with a 5-10 year maturity, depending on project requirements. The majority of local financial markets are not yet developed enough to provide loans to local businesses. Further, the capacity of local banks to offer the:

- maturity
- pricing
- and volumes

are well below the needs to bring clean energy access projects to fruition at the scale intended.

It is important to note that loan maturities provided by local banks average around 3 years and therefore typically cannot meet the loan maturity requirements of 7–10 years of the proposed investments. This is largely due to the fact that the largest funding source for local banks are short-term liabilities, the majority of which are demand deposits. The GCF support will provide longer term financing through its own balance sheet. In addition, UEGAP therefore provides much-needed long-term funding for local banks, allowing them to extend loans to energy supply projects with a longer maturity.

- Consistent data on the asset-liability mismatch of local banks is not available as banks differ in their positions.
- However, two examples represent the situation well:

In both countries, the weighted average life of liabilities fluctuates between 2–3 years – which is in significant contrast to the 7-year average life of assets that UEGAP anticipates to invest into.

In terms of pricing, even in case loans are available with the required maturities, local currency loans provided by local banks typically have a higher interest rate than those provided by international financial institutions. In the projects in question, GCF's involvement in UEGAP will allow local banks to extend loans with interest rates of 8–10% (USD equivalent) which are affordable vis-à-vis the expected return of the proposed investments.

### Table 31: Key Parameters of Financial Markets

Given that the proposed investments will be a new asset class for international investors and in light of their regulatory investment constraints, international investors alone cannot be counted on to invest with matures above 3-5 years to ensure that UGEAP can extend long-term debt finance to local FIs. A long term tranche from GCF with the proposing 15-year maturity will allow UGEAP to meet its financing needs and ensure that UGEAP can provide debt finance with long-term maturities to meet the financing demand of local energy access projects.

### Triggering private investment at scale through the appropriate risk-return profile

The objective of UGEAP is to multiply the funding provided by the GCF by a factor of 2-3 through large scale debt investment from international private sector investors. This requires an appropriate risk-return profile, including risk mitigation for both parties involved.

### In terms of risk profile, the actual and perceived risks of the proposed investments are very high from a private investor perspective, including lack of familiarity with the risk profile of local African FIs and energy supply companies, as well as the limited availability of credit information on these entities. This is compounded by the principal risks. These risks are too high to allow for a straight exposure to the underlying portfolio which does not meet the investment criteria of most private investors. Therefore, it will be necessary that GCF provide debt finance available without risk mitigation provided by the GCF. This risk mitigation consists of GCF's investment in UGEAP's subordinated equity and/or debt instruments. The purpose is to allow for a structured approach to attract private investors with a risk buffer. This will allow private sector investors to become comfortable with the risk profile and its default probabilities.


---
**Page 84**
---

In terms of return expectations, debt investors require stable returns being paid on their capital that should also be linked to floating rate returns in order to stabilize the present value of their investment while yield curves change. UEGAP proposes to private sector investors to have access to the total amount of interest income from the investments in the following way:

1) 1.0 share in the return stream (after having paid senior operating expenses);
2) 1.4 shares in the returns that are left after the return rates on the Class B capital has been paid.

The volatility in African economies is deemed to be outstandingly high and single defaults may occur without creditors being able to recover their principal. This can be mitigated by diversification, which can be achieved by inspecting "quality like" returns above 20% that projects UEGAP targets cannot provide for debt investors.
GCF's involvement as risk cushion investor is therefore needed to stabilize the return profile of the A-tranche by removing the credit risk. This will also reduce the cost of capital and increase the attractiveness of UEGAP to private sector investors and lower the probability of write-offs for private investors which would negatively impact UEGAP's ability to raise funds in the future. The GCF is a long-term investor with a strong track record of providing stable returns, acceptance for portfolio investments are realistic and DB believes that a significant portion of the capital refinancing the underlying assets will be provided by the GCF based on its experience with similar structures.

All alternative sources of public sector funding are also available.
Given the large investment needs and opportunities estimated in total at USD 500m, a sizeable amount is required as investment into the subordinated B-tranche which currently only the GCF is able to provide within the given timeframe. This is due to the fact that the GCF has a long-term horizon and can therefore invest in projects with longer payback periods. Changes in adequately programming the proposed structure which is a hybrid of both risk and direct project risk; no single DFI has the capacity to finance this transaction requires a longer time frame.

In addition, UEGAP establishes GCF as leading investor in infrastructure finance as no other program to date has managed to combine the elements of developing local financial markets and tapping into large scale international debt investment for the benefit of emerging markets. In Africa's governance in the B-tranche of UEGAP triggers this innovation.
Capacity of banks to underwrite projects is also a concern.

The transactions UEGAP targets are not in the focus of financial institutions yet which has various reasons. Most banks have raised the concern that their in-house experience to deal with (a) consumer based lending in rural areas and (b) long-term financing for infrastructure projects is limited. This is further complicated by the fact that they lack experienced consultants. Further DB sees the need to build capacity across the sector by connecting financial institutions amongst each other. This will not only strengthen the capacity of banks but also produce competition which would improve the availability of funding for businesses UEGAP targets to promote.

Once the business case has been proven, sustainability for UEGAP can be achieved by private sector investors

Investing in the A-branche with bigger machines and also in the higher yielding B-branches, so that UGEAP would continue operations without the need for a public sector risk taker. It would be an expectation that investors would include African pension funds, insurance companies and other local institutional investors at that point in time.

Alternatively, as the investment proposition associated investments requirements and electricity access situation can be envisaged to evolve over time, it is possible that the following conditions will have been met on the development management pathways:

• Local financial institutions would have developed sufficient capabilities, investment expertise and long-term funding sources to finance the energy supply businesses by themselves without the need for a financial contribution from UGEAP;

• other international capital market investors start investing into the sector UGEAP covers;

• local financial markets would have developed in terms of depth and the capital available which translates into an increased number of investors, including pension funds and insurance companies;

• businesses under category 3 investments would be in a position to finance their clean energy access from their own resources without the need for a public sector risk taker;

• the wealth of End Beneficiaries has increased such that they can afford investments in clean energy sources from their own resources, now without the need to make use of the energy supply companies UGEAP will invest into, which may be more uneconomic.

In addition, the business case for UGEAP will need to be re-assessed after 15 years in light of the then applicable national electrification strategy.

> «Degree to which activity avoids lock-in of long-lived, high-emission infrastructure»

## Details and background of the impact potential of UGEP's investment activity

Energy access is key for the development of Sub-Saharan Africa. UGEP avoids the lock-in of high-emission infrastructures in the African electricity supply for households and communities (Categories 1 and 2 Projects) as well as for industrial and commercial customers (Category 3 Projects) in their development pathway of granting people access to a reliable energy supply and to allow for economic growth by serving local industrial and commercial customers.

Given that the energy mix in the target region already heavily depends on fossil-fuel based electricity and/or the target region does not provide for reliable grid infrastructure, further adding high-emission diesel generators to power distribution networks is not an option. The only way to avoid lock-in of high-emission infrastructures is by providing renewable energy.

Both, low-carbon energy production and the expansion of rural energy services can be achieved by scaling up the use of renewable energy sources, particularly photovoltaic in combination with small battery kits and hybrid systems which is the focus of UGEP's investment activity.

The first two project categories focus electing local communities and homes (Category 1 and 2 Projects) and Category 3 Projects are providing renewable energy for businesses. The additional benefits, beyond CO2 emission reduction:

- **The provision of clean, safe and sustainable electricity sources providing ability for communities to have home appliances, lighting, refrigeration, cooking, etc., which in turn increases access to education and communications;**
- **Access to charging units creates multiple benefits for households, such as availability of several hours of electricity per day, increased income generation through mobile phone recharging, job creation, availability of lighting for studying purposes, reduced health impacts from dirty cooking fuels, etc.**
- **At the community level, increased resilience, safety, empowerment and sustainability as a result of access to lighting and appliances; improved quality of life in terms of better education and communications, provision of improved health care services and in community centers with a positive impact on community empowerment and decision-making**

- Renewable power increases access to telecommunication resulting in a reduced travel demand, thereby reducing fossil fuel consumption and also connects people better to markets, therefore improving their livelihoods.
- If any of the power generated by these projects is used to run refrigeration equipment this will help the local population to store food more effectively. This will improve treatment against infectious diseases including Malaria and other vector borne diseases.
- Given economic growth rates and increased need for electricity for the economy, hybrid systems for industries will play a key role in reducing CO2 emissions generated by different productive sectors of the African economies.

| Lack/Unreliability of grid connection | Energy access/supply in Africa is limited due to limited coverage by electricity grids, driving up the high emission from diesel generation use. | Two models: 1) Off-grid solar photovoltaic/battery kits as well as highly-efficient PV hybrid systems with batteries are offered. This business model bypasses the need of a grid infrastructure |
| Familiarity and trust in renewable technology | Lack of technical and commercial know-how which is necessary for the roll out. Limited knowledge about installation, maintenance and operation of renewable energy installations | Program supports trust-building by rolling out pilot concepts with a proven technology. Capacity building of local staff as well as end users |
| High transaction costs | Initial capital investment increases financing costs as well as installation costs | Program benefits from economies of scale in all projects Due to aggregation of different projects, development programme funding from private sector is facilitated Investment of local bank transaction costs can be reduced |

### Category 1 Benchmark Investment
- Projects of this category provide off-grid SHS to rural, remote and vulnerable communities in one country, thereby contributing to both sustainable and low-emission development pathways for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
    - Projects provide access to sustainable energy sources and lighting for households that previously relied on conventional energy supplies such as diesel generator sets (as a shared energy source) or kerosene lamps (for individual use).
    - Within 2015, the sample project analyzed has the capacity to electrify at least 4.5 million households that previously depended solely on fuel for lighting, resulting in a total of 4.7 MWh installed renewable energy capacity.
    
### Category 2 Benchmark Investment
- This project provides mini-grid PV solutions to villages, thus contributing to both sustainable and low-emission development pathways for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
    - The sample project analyzed is estimated to electrify around 13.0 million customers, resulting in additional income of 1/3 of a total of 41,500 SHS systems results in 12,500 households less vulnerable to climate change by diversifying their tourism-related activities.
    - Larger-scale SHS kits for productive activities can also offer an alternative to local income-generating activities from agriculture, making them more resilient to future climate change impacts such as droughts.

### Category 3 Benchmark Investment
- Projects in this category replace CO2-intensive energy production from diesel generators with low-emission photovoltaic systems and integrate them into a hybrid system for local industries with ensuing climate change mitigation benefits:
    - The sample project analyzed is estimated to electrify around 84.8 million households, contributing to an additional income of 1/3 of the total electricity capacity and avoiding approximately 5,183 tCO2 within the first three years.
    - Solar power mini-grids also improve access to reliable electricity for increased productive activities through concentration of production units in SME companies, resulting in increased household income and resilience.
    - Technology progress allows for a full service package including household connection, metering, pay-per-use charges, and local after-sales maintenance services, increasing the reliability and availability of electricity service while keeping the operations cost and capital expenditures low.

## Other relevant indicators

### Description for detailed methodology used for calculating the indicators above:
Energy and carbon savings have been calculated for each project analysed in accordance with the following principles:

* **Methodology:** Where actual production data is not available (Project 1 and Project 2), the project appropriate standard methodologies have been followed as outlined in the Green Climate Fund Methodology Booklet.
* **Baseline:** The Bank has followed the CDM standardized baseline methodologies to facilitate calculation of emission reduction for proposed project activities as frameworks include parameters specific to the electricity generation sector. These methodologies ensure consistency, comparability, transparency, objectivity and predictability of each project's energy and carbon savings while adopting simplification, reporting and verification approach.
* **Project Activities:** Projects with chosen CDM technology/measure criteria as stated below:
    * Electricity generation and supply will be through the installation of new, renewable electricity generation systems (e.g. solar photovoltaic panels that form a grid set-up such as household lighting systems and stand-alone power generators).
    * Generative electric use includes interior lighting, street lighting, agriculture water pumping, refrigeration, air conditioning, heating, mobile phones, TVs, etc.
    * At least 75% of the end-use facilities connected to the project renewable electricity generation system(s) through a grid or mini-grid.
* **Project equipment shall comply with international standards or comparable national, regional or local guidelines will be a fixed system where each system will not exceed 13.5kW.

## GREEN FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 91 OF 165

- To calculate baseline emissions: the amount of renewable electricity consumed by the facilities served by the project renewable electricity generation systems and the number of facilities (e.g. households) supplied with renewable electricity.
- Static data for project 1 and 2:
    - The following are the baseline emission factors for each branch of annual actual consumption:
        o For the first 50 kWh of renewable electricity consumed by each facility, the baseline emission factor is 6.8 (tCO2/kWh).
        o For the facility consumption greater than 55 kWh but equal to or less than 250 kWh, the baseline emission factor is 1.3 tCO2/MWh.
        o For the facility consumption beyond 250 kWh, the baseline emission factor is 1.0 t CO2/MWh for the tranche between 250 and kWh.

- Static equation for project 1 and 2: Baseline emissions for the entire project activity are then calculated as:
    - BE = BE1 + BE2 + BE3 + BE4
Where:
    - BE1: Baseline emissions in year y (tCO2)
    - BE2: Aggregated baseline emissions for facilities that consumed equal to or less than 55 kWh of renewable electricity from project renewable electricity systems in year y (t CO2)
    - BE3: Aggregated baseline emissions for facilities that consumed greater than 55 kWh but equal to or less than 250 kWh of renewable electricity from project renewable electricity systems in year y (t CO2)
    - BE4: Aggregated baseline emissions for facilities that consumed greater than 250 kWh of renewable electricity from project renewable electricity systems in year y (t CO2)

- Energy savings calculation equation for project 1 and project 2:
    - Energy Savings = Baseline * Efficiency * Hours / Availability
Where:
    - Baseline: Baseline emissions
    - Hours: 8760 (365 days × 24 hours)
    - Availability: 12% (UNFCCC assumes 12% availability for solar photovoltaic electricity systems. Account for daily UNFCCC availability can only be assumed for installed capacities of each project renewable electricity generation system being less or equal to 1.0 kW. DBS assumes the availability ratio (12%) accounts for daily solar radiation, solar panel efficiency of conversion and performance ratio of solar panel).
- Key document reference: Clean Development Mechanism (CDM), 2014e. Detailed in footnote**.

- The project is for solar domestic use which replaces a mix of off-grid fuel sources including diesel generators, kerosene lamps and battery operated lights.
- The project is to power a variety of electrical appliances including LED lighting and battery re-charging.
- The project will be implemented in Tanzania and Rwanda (2014).
- The number of panels equates to 94,380 households being connected to devices by the end of 2020 (end of UCEAP project phase). The assumption is one device equating to one panel and one device reaching 7 households is used.

- Emission conversion factors are taken from CDM (2014a).
- For 80 Wp devices the emissions reduction factor is of **0.8 tCO2e/MWh** for the first 55 kWh per device and **1.3 tCO2e/MWh** for a capacity between 55 kWh and 250 kWh per device, the following factors are derived:
- For 90 Wp * 8760 / 8760 = 0.12 - 0.09 MWh / yr (device). Baseline emissions: (0.09 - 0.055) * 1.3 + (0.055 * 6.8)
- For 100 Wp * 8760 / 8760 = 0.14 MWh / yr (device). Baseline emissions: (0.09 - 0.055) * 1.3 + (0.055 * 6.8)

### E.2. Paradigm Shift Potential

#### E.2.1. Paradigm shift potential can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project/program investment.

- **Potential for scaling up and replication (Provide a numerical multiple and supporting rationale):**
  - *Desired expected contributions to global low-carbon and/or climate-resilient development pathways through a theory of change on scaling up and replication (e.g. in terms of multiples of initial impact of the proposed project/program).*

## Program Level

**UGEAP will leverage GCF resources by a factor of 1:3.3 as part of the proposed program, creating the first program for public-private climate finance in Africa at scale, with a target volume of USD 500 million. In addition, successful investments under UGEAP will be eligible to access additional funding from other sources and the program as new markets have been created which will move towards sustainability and continue beyond UGEAP.**

**UGEAP contributes to the paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways through the following paradigm shifts:**

- **Mobilizing private climate finance in partnership and on scale for climate-smart electricity access investments:** Private sector capital can be invested alongside existing local capacity from project developers as well as local banks to the benefit of climate-smart energy access projects. Private sector capital is not currently available or is too expensive to flow into climate-relevant mitigation projects (like those UGEAP targets) on the African continent and therefore do not cover the region with the necessary scale. On the other hand, the current demand for debt businesses in the targeted categories is not met by local financial institutions.
- **Paradigm shift: UGEAP provides the first integrated platform to link private sector investors, local financial institutions, and local project developers on a single platform to unlock universal access in Africa. UGEAP - on the active side - will indirectly add operational assets to the balance sheets of local banks and enable them to serve longer transactions that are not reachable with their own capital alone. On the passive side, UGEAP provides an opportunity for investors who have the opportunity to tap the climate mitigation market in Africa with higher certainty. Successful investments through UGEAP will also provide a track record for other investors who will allocate (or offer) their own capital once they built up a successful track-record in lending to these projects from international investors who will feel more comfortable to invest at scale after early successes and track records are established.
- **Innovation through local and international financial market development:** At the local level, UGEAP will provide local banks with long-term funding for cash flow-based lending models. At the international level, UGEAP provides the first platform in Africa that can unlock universal access to electricity across Africa. Both markets have local banks in Africa as an asset-liability mismatch: in many African economies, financial markets are still predominantly short term; in some cases even without a long-term financing benchmark (such as long-term government bonds). UGEAP will provide the first platform for international investors to invest in long-term lending to projects which require a longer time horizon.


---
**Page 98**
---

In addition, local banks' lending practices are not conducive to financing new technologies. In mature renewable energy markets financial institutions finance renewable energy projects of all sizes in cash-flow-based (project) finance arrangements. The same is true for the projects in the Target Countries where most project operations mostly against hard collateral (the value of which is often higher than the loan amount) but is not yet open to grant loans for which the repayment relies on the cash that is produced by companies' operations. In addition, banks are reluctant to lend to small and medium-sized enterprises.

In an emerging economy, a debt-to-EBITDA multiple of 1-2 times the earnings minus net operational costs (i.e. before interest, tax and depreciation) (DEBTAT), is typical. Businesses with high turnover (trade, agriculture, retail, manufacturing) can easily meet this requirement as their cash flow from operations comfortably fall into that target range.

This is in contrast to climate businesses that are producing less cash (and are less profitable) and can offer less hard collateral than the average business in Africa. In order for these projects to produce adequate returns, 8-10 times EBITDA is a typical requirement for the amount of required debt at interest rates that are typically higher than those available on conventional loans.

The local banks in Africa are not able to provide such financing due to:

o Lack of experience with new type of structure/underwriting

o Not foreseen in the credit procedures of the local financial institutions;

o Long term execution horizons while other business (such as real estate) allows to earn higher interest rates;

o Perception of high risk - low return combination.

In order to overcome these obstacles, development banks can make it unattractive to start lending to these businesses.


On the side of international investors they currently do not invest in green electricity access projects in Africa directly or via local financial institutions because perceived risks are too high, including political, market, technology and operating risks and the expected returns to compensate for such risks cannot be provided by the projects.

Paradigm Shift: UGEEP creates both new markets for local banks as well as investors by overcoming the barriers that currently exist:

* Providing long-term debt finance to local financial institutions which can then intermediated into long-term loans to small and medium-sized enterprises with green energy access projects.
* Partnering with local banks in project analysis and underwriting, thereby building their capacity in cash-flow based lending to these projects
* Providing a risk-sharing mechanism for international investors to start investing at scale in renewable energy access projects in Africa

Building up both these local and international financial markets and creating a growing capital flow through them will drive progress for capital interest rates down given that the average returns will now come from these projects.


Innovation to the traditional "green bonds" - Providing long-term debt to financial institutions in an existing market structure, so as to enable banks to provide green energy capital into specifically targeted sectors. While this leaves banks with the risk of risk, a risk participation provides an even stronger incentive to serve industries and sectors that are currently not covered by the risk bank.


In order to mobilize funding for local projects within the target categories of the UGEEP, banks are expected to provide loans to sectors that are currently outside of their target and comfort zone. Local economies are evolving very quickly which is also reflected in lending standards. Banks tend to show high green rates and


---
**Page 99**
---

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 97 OF 165

growth requires equity and profitability. Profitability in the markets is high as banks are able to charge relatively high fees and interest from developed market business segments. The target businesses need, however, interest rates that are at the top of the offering range for banks to be willing to lend to them. Banks are also reluctant to expand into—although their business models come with significant growth prospects from the local banks.

In addition, lending standards of local banks generally are not adapted to the business types UGAEAP targets to finance. Local banks have a limited appetite for risk and are more inclined to lend to larger companies or a smaller number of small energy companies. In order to expand the lending practices, local risk procedures need to adjust to the new business models. The financial sector is also reluctant to take on the risks in the take-over by UGAEAP; the offer provides sufficient incentive to initiate the transition.

Paradigm Shift: UGAEAP allows local banks to extend their scope of services and customer outreach, supports the financial inclusion agenda, and reduces the risk for banks to lend. The take-over is also a win-win for local banks towards the businesses that are typical for an economy backed by many de-centralized green energy projects.

UGAEAP delivers long-term capital through debt: While international investors focus on equity investments in the Target Region, UGAEAP offers long term debt which supports local entrepreneurs and stabilizes national account balances.

Typically, investments by international investors (tracked by the 'foreign direct investment' statistics) come in with a relatively short term orientation and the ability to divest in case macroeconomic conditions turn negative. Further, they are not willing to take on the risks of long-term debt financing or provide the necessary equity investors in order to fund the capital expenditures required to grow the businesses in line with the market potential. This reduces the amount of cap-side returns and limits local entrepreneurs can retain. Debt is also used to reduce the financial costs of a company and therefore makes businesses feasible that otherwise would not succeed.

In addition, on a national level, equity investments leaving the economy in case of a downturn lead to a further de-localization of capital flows. UGAEAP's debt financing will allow for capital to flow "only" on expected terms.

Paradigm Shift: Provide long-term, stable and foreseeable capital contributions to the expanding economic activity in the Target Region rather than short term oriented capital flows.

**Sustainability and Scalability:** Renewable energy is a competitive, scalable technology:

Businesses and households are still skeptical toward renewable energy, particularly toward photovoltaics, in Sub-Saharan Africa which is perceived to be:
- Expensive
- Unreliable
- Unproven
- High maintenance

compared with the traditional Diesel based alternative which they are familiar with.

Paradigm Shift: UGAEAP will scale up successful renewable energy access models in off-grid, mini-grid and industry solutions. UGAEAP act based on private sector, market-based principles and only finance transactions that have a high probability of success. The financial model is designed for an investment horizon of five years which will not allow not investing in the first of its kind of projects under commercial terms, but also supply capital to the market follows. This will create multiple benefits, including:
- Financial sustainability: UGAEAP will only finance projects that have a high probability of success.

## Project Level

- Combining sustainable economic growth and climate change mitigation: rising economic growth rates further increase the demand, while small business entrepreneurship in remote areas can be served especially well by grid-based electrification supply linking small businesses and the country's general economic growth to renewable energy sources;
