- Sustainability and Scalability: All installations are highly standardized and are delivered in very short time, i.e. installation times for a village mini-grid typically take only one week to set up a village mini-grid after approval by the village council. Business models are all based on value of the simple technology and the very good applicability in the national context.
- Financial service: All investors of the UGEP provide indirect financial services to end users which removes the need for high upfront investments while local banks are reluctant to finance on long terms.

## E.2.3. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

Describe how proposed measures will create conditions that are conducive to effective and sustained participation of private sector actors in sector action in low-carbon or resilient development?

### Program Level

- **Technological sustainability**: UGEAP offers long term capital only to businesses that mitigate greenhouse gas emissions. Promoting the respective technologies and scaling them up as the basis for economic activities is a key element in building resilience in the energy sector, which is part of sustainable development. In addition, after amortization of initial investments, renewable energy is almost free from fuel costs and has a very low maintenance cost. This makes it more competitive advantages for its users and empowerment at the local level due to self-sustainability.
- **Accelerated local development path**: Investments in all Categories deliver climate-smart infrastructure assets that are designed to be resilient against extreme weather events and based energy carriers. Current projections suggest that local electric energy grids are far too low to supply electricity to all households, especially in rural areas. UGEAP will support the transition towards a decentralized generation mix. The UGEAP therefore accelerates the expected development path for local economies and will provide them with the necessary infrastructure to implement their own renewable energy strategies.
- **Environmental and social sustainability**: All investments of UGEAP have to be in line with strict social, environmental and governance standards. Hence climate-smart businesses can be showcases for local regulatory authorities and other stakeholders who are interested in replicating these business models therefore facilitate supportive local regulation for clean energy infrastructure solutions.

### Building local markets

- **Building local financial markets**: All processes to be executed within the framework of UGEAP have to have local maintenance and operations (with global sourcing of technology and components) thereby mainstreaming and disseminating knowledge and capacity on photovoltaic technology into African energy supply.
- **Building local financial markets for climate finance**: By partnering with local banks, UGEAP builds the capacity of these institutions to provide financing to small-scale businesses. Once local conditions allow this business model, they are expected to continue providing finance to these businesses even without UGEAP participation.

### Project Level

Targets investors that offer solar-home-systems as well as mini-grids support conditions that are conducive to effective and sustained participation of mainly low-income populations towards low-carbon development by

## Category 3 Projects

Energy supply for industrial used mainly delivers towards the target to improve the operational conditions for companies while two sectors can serve as examples:

### Agri-processing sector
- **Farming operations** as well as third-party suppliers to SME agro-processors, like private farmers, including out-growers and smallholder farmers, prefer to have their produce processed on an environmental friendly site at a reasonable cost.

- All parties involved in the supply chain will benefit from cost efficient and environmental friendly produce.

### Mining sector
- **Mine operators** are increasingly aware of their ecological footprint and of their image which suffered from recent incidents and the media.
- It is an observation that many mine operators across sub-Saharan Africa are reviving their Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) programs, with a focus on:
  - The improvement of health conditions for their workers: a major ambition within this context is to reduce the prevalence of respiratory diseases. This can be achieved through better air quality and well-being measures – the reduction of CO2 emissions can reduce the prevalence of respiratory diseases.
  - The reduction of their carbon footprint through hybridized power production equipment fits very well into this context.
- Due to declining prices on global mineral markets, mine operators also need to be more cost-effective than ever before. This is where energy efficiency and renewable energy solutions become increasingly viable solutions:
  - UGECAP will carefully and diligently choose which mine would be served based on what only consider priority energy solutions for mining companies which the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards that will be reflected in UGECAP own standards.

#### E2.4 Contribution to regulatory framework and policies

**Program Level**

LIGEAP will strengthen the national/local regulatory or legal frameworks to systematically drive investment in low-emission technologies or activities by applying the following measures:
- Invest only in projects that are economically sustainable and therefore do not require subsidies;
- Where relevant, will work with local regulators (and other donors as applicable) to create simplified application procedures for such investments.
- Will contribute to increasing the share renewables have in the respective national energy mix and national electricity generation.

Upon their demand, they will work with government planning authorities/economic development agencies (and other donors as applicable) to promote scaling up renewable energy solutions as basis for industrial and other economic activities. They will also provide technical assistance to governments, local authorities, financial institutions or other investors into the projects.
- Provides access to financing for projects that use the best technology along its SAE standards;
- Reduces through its investments the requirement to import/produce fossil fuel carriers and therefore reduces existing trade deficits that are caused by the import of refined oil products;
- Invests in projects that will provide additional income to households, including household income as well as tax revenues; and
- Works closely with national legislation and international best practice, e.g. regarding licensing, taxation/import duties or social and environmental standards, and therefore strengthens national frameworks.

### Economic Co-Benefits

EUGAP and its three project categories generate the following wider economic co-benefits:

- **Total number of jobs created:** These include:
  - The number of direct permanent and non-permanent employment created by the program, including a growing share of women in the workforce as outlined in section H.
  - Additional employment created through entrepreneurial activities and an increased productivity created as a result of the projects. For example, in Project 1, 200 people were employed to install solar panels for 500 customers; in Project 1 on off-grid regions started a business by selling their excess energy produced each day.
- **Indirect employment created:** In suppliers to EUGAP's investment projects and through the increased spending power from the GDP contribution of EUGAP's investments.

#### Amount of foreign currency savings

Most Sub-Saharan countries depend on the import of Diesel as there are no national refining capacities. This drives the trade deficit, as imports increase subject to the volatility of oil prices and further weight on the need to process foreign currency inflows into the country to finance the trade deficit.

Based on a conversion factor of 1 USD = 0.538 Euros, the amount of production stemming from the three energy transformation alone could save 8.8 Mtonnes of Diesel. At an assumed price of 0.50USD per tonne, the contribution from the three existing businesses would amount to USD4.8m in savings annually.

Scaling up the impact with EUGAP's projects, a total annual saving of 60.135Mtonnes as detailed in section E and H, an annual saving of production costs of 52.371 Mtonnes of Diesel could be saved if all installations were to replace Diesel generators. This would reduce the annual import bill by USD48m annually.

The savings from the three existing businesses alone exceed total import funding requirements by USD523bn – more than the amount UGAP targets to invest in the economies.

#### Poverty reduction

As a result of growing employment and household income, it is expected that EUGAP contributes to decreasing poverty rates and extreme poverty, in the local communities and regions where its investments take place.

- **Improved access to education:** Through better lighting, children can study more hours through new jobs and increased productivity
- **New opportunities for leisure (through time savings, TV and lighting):**

# Program Level

## Economic and Social Development

UGEAP addresses the following needs for social and economic development:

- Access to electrical energy (Category I and Z projects) of populations and businesses that have no access to electricity: UGEAP contributes to increasing access to electricity, especially in rural areas and low-income communities and in the near future. UGEAP hence contributes to delivering national electrification targets to increase the share of population with access to electrical energy from 20% today to 45% by 2030 and also increase household income through new entrepreneurial activity with positive benefits for poverty reduction.
- For Category 3 projects: improve electrical energy supply, increase productivity and reduce costs, improve local health services, and provide alternative power generation options such as small-scale diesel generators or hybrid electric generation or diesel replacement with renewable energy technology.

All projects reduce further the dependency of the population towards Diesel. Hence, the projects also have the benefit of reducing:

- Trade deficits of affected countries and generating foreign currency savings;
- World regional ecological costs from transport-related accidents.

The above benefits have significant co-benefits as laid out in section E.3 above and also portrayed in the three sample transactions chosen and described below.


## Absence of Alternative Sources of Financing

Local currency debt funding with the required tenors and interest rates is scarce to non-existent for the businesses identified, even though their investments are economically and financially profitable. UGEAP works through and with local banks, financial institutions taking over risks which allow the local financial institutions to unlock access to the required debt capital for the sector.


## Need for Strengthening Institutions and Implementation Capacity

Local financial institutions are strengthened and increase capacity by working along with UGEAP on transactions that fall into the respective categories. Strengthening is further achieved by supplying long-term liquid term capital to the local banks which facilitate the financing of projects in the pipeline. The strengthening of the local financial institutions also ensures that the risk transfer to UGEAP allows for a more efficient use of the available equity of the local financial institutions, which also generates higher taxable returns of the local banks.

In addition to providing technical assistance and capacity building for households and industry, UGEAP contributes towards the capacity of local governments and regulators to understand the benefits of off-grid and mini-grid solar power solutions as well as other solutions from renewable energy sources and to improve the enabling framework for such transactions implemented by the private sector.


## Category I and Z Projects

These projects contribute to the economic and social development level of the country and the affected population, especially as these relate to:

- The need for electrification: Serving rural households with electricity, otherwise being off-grid/relying on high-

These projects contribute to the economic and social development level of the country and the affected population, especially as these relate to:

- **Increased access** to easy deployable and removable PV systems driving economic development in remote off-grid areas, reducing dependence on Diesel and improving health conditions.
In respect to strengthening financing institutions and growing implementation capacity:

- Strengthening the understanding of the value added of renewable energy based power supply on the side of commercial customers;
- Training of local service providers for installation, operations and maintenance strengthens the local implementation capacity.

Through all of the above, investments in Categories 1, 2 and 3 will not just contribute to socio-economic development, but also to Millennium Development Goals 7a (as regards the reduction in carbon dioxide emissions) and 8f (as regards benefit from access to energy for all). They are also aligned with Goal 6 (access to clean water and sanitation for all") and Goal 13 "Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impact" of the new 2030 Sustainable Development Goals, adopted by world leaders in September 2015.

### Contribution (INDC)

**Source:** Deutsche Bank

#### Benin

Benin submitted its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) on 7 August 2015, committing to lower emissions in its production, energy consumption, transport, and agriculture and forestry sectors. The country signed up to adapt its national climate change strategy to the Paris Agreement and committed to implementing necessary mitigation measures. Thereewith, the country offers to cut 5 Mt from the energy sector and 115 Mt from the land use and land-use change sector between the years 2020-2030. This shall be achieved within the framework of the national targets that promote the installation of newly installed photovoltaic capacity which will ultimately lead to the electrification of 1000 communities.

Benin commits to improve energy access by using renewable energy sources in all sectors, especially in rural areas and fields of agriculture. The country is planning on a priority to validate renewable energy projects at large scale through newly created national agency for the development of renewable energies (AMADAE) plans to create 105 solar mini-grids for villages not connected to the national grid. Renewable energy development measures foreseen among others the implementation of 1 million solar home systems and 200,000 biogas digesters. In addition, the country is planning on the installation of 300 MW of solar energy is planned to be used for the extraction of salt in order to restore the country's fragile ecosystems such as mangroves.

#### Namibia

The goal of Namibia’s Vision 2030 is a “prosperous and industrialized Namibia, developed by her human resources, enjoying peace, harmony and political stability”. The National Development Plan which serves as the implementation framework for Vision 2030 identified 5 “Basic Enablers” which are critical to achieve this vision, including reducing extreme poverty and unemployment. The government has set out a number of strategies in order to support industry development through the construction of energy infrastructure and the production capacity from 400 to more than 750 megawatts by 2030.

To achieve this goal the government will “promote the use of economically viable renewable technologies, as a complement to grid electrification, to improve energy provision to rural areas”. As women represent over 50% of the population, Vision 2030 aims at providing them with access to affordable and reliable energy services.

Namibia has set itself an Off-Grid Energy Master Plan (OGEMP) and the Ministry of Environment and Tourism and have recently issued the Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Action (NAMA): Rural Development in Namibia through electrification and access to renewable energies, namely to provide access to appropriate energy technologies to everyone living or working in off-grid areas.

In its INDC, Namibia undertakes to Namibia aims at a reduction of about 89% of its GHG emissions at the 2030 time line

## Nigeria

Nigeria's Vision20:20 aims to launch Nigeria on a path towards becoming a strong, diversified, sustainable and competitive economy by 2020. The country has set out its vision for energy security, economic growth and industries is a cornerstone to achieve this vision and meet the needs of Nigeria’s 140 million inhabitants. Vision20:20 also undertakes to promote the use of “green technology” to ensure sustainable production in the electricity and agricultural sectors.

Nigeria offers it in its INDC a pledge to reduce its emissions by 20% from business as usual levels in 2030, rising to a possible 45% contingent upon adequate international support. The country continues to work towards off-grid solar photovoltaic (PV) deployment and the development of a national grid.

Nigeria, which became the largest economy in sub-Saharan Africa in 2014, chose to adopt these renewable energy measures in its national development agenda in order to prevent incurring costs that would result from exacerbated climate change. With regard to this, the renewable energy measures would lead to a potential 37m tonnes reduction of GHG per year in 2030.

Nigeria is host to a number of Clean Development Mechanism projects, as well as projects financed by the Adaptation Fund. Within its National Climate Change Action Plan and the National Renewable Energy Policy and Strategy in September 2012 thus underlining the efforts that the country is undertaking in the field of renewable energy

## Tanzania

According to its national development plan, Vision 2050, Tanzania seeks to “graduate from a least developed country to a middle income country by 2035 with a high level of human development”. Scaling up investments in energy and transport infrastructure will be key to achieving this goal. The government has identified the need for the country to fast track the provision of the basic conditions for broad-based and pro-growth, with a targeted GDP growth rate of 8% p.a., by 2035. This is expected to require an average annual investment in energy infrastructure of US$1 billion per year.

The main source of energy in Tanzania is biomass (fuel-wood and charcoal) which accounts for about 65% of the total primary energy supply, followed by hydroelectricity at around 24%. Fossil fuels account for only 9%, while electricity accounts for 0.7% of total energy consumption. So far very few alternative renewable energy resources, such as mini-hydro, wind, solar and geothermal have been commercially exploited, despite their potential availability in the country. The national plan calls for a significant increase in the share of renewables in the overall energy mix.

The national plan therefore seeks to work on new sources of energy production across the country. The proposed interventions target the development of mini-hydro power plants, solar and wind farms, as well as geothermal projects.

The Plan also calls for additional emphasis on scaling up rural electrification for district headquarters, townships, villages, and commercial centres as the basis for improved growth, economic development, and enhanced living standards.

Consequently, the plan envisages that by 2035, Tanzania will have a climate-resilient economy with added social and gender considerations, as both play a crucial role in achieving the goals of Vision 2050. Preservation of the rich ecological base of Tanzania is also seen as an important element for ensuring sustainable development and long-term economic growth.

The costs from droughts is expected to be as high as 2% of GDP by 2035 and rising to sea level rise of lost of 274 km² of land is forecast. Therefore, environmental concerns will be mainstreamed in all future policy measures and herefore they will be given utmost priority along with climate-wise economic development policies.

Tanzania has put a comprehensive framework in place to develop transparent sector regulation, promote rural electrification and increase private sector investment in Tanzania’s energy projects.

Key elements of the framework include: electricity supply under Tanzania Electricity Supply Act and Water Utilities Authority Act, 2001 and 2006; National Energy Policy, 2003; Rural Energy Act, 2005; Electricity Act, 2008; and Public Private Partnership Act No. 18, 2009.

The Renewable Energy Agency (REA) is processing additional co-funding to prepare for 60 stand-alone solar projects and mini-grid projects to benefit more consumers, also preparing a Rural Electrification Investment Prospectus that is to be launched in the first quarter of 2013. The REA is also planning with renewable energy-based mini-grid and stand-alone electrification options.

The Ministry of Energy and Minerals (MEM) and the Rural Energy Agency (REA) have policies and procedures in place for projects with social and environmental impacts.

The National Electrification Program Projection estimates that about half of the rural population might be the most cost-effective option to electrify. The projection shows that 50% of the rural population will be electrified by grid and 32% from stand-alone and mini-grid PV. The projections will define a least-cost investment plan for 2013–22, aiming at achieving universal access in Tanzania.

The Electrification Investment Prospectus will define the least cost path for grid electrification and will identify those districts and communities that cannot be cost-effectively reached by grid and should be targeted for private sector-driven, off-grid electrification investments. The prospectus will also provide a framework to support investment in mini-grids and stand-alone systems.

The three top priority choices that emerged to support national development priorities were geothermal power development, solar PV, and biomass energy. Geothermal power development is the most cost-effective option for Tanzania. The Government of Tanzania decided to focus on the first two priorities, with the understanding that additional SREP resources, if they become available, would be used to implement the strategy to emerge from the Biomass Energy Strategy into a more comprehensive renewable energy strategy.

The National Climate Change Strategy will integrate the climate change dimension into national policies and programs.

Kenya Vision 2030 aims to transform Kenya into a newly industrializing, "middle-income country providing a high quality life to all its citizens by the year 2030." A focus on energy: science, technology and innovation underpins the Vision as strategic pillar. The National Energy and Petroleum Policy clearly states that the range of development projects identified under Vision 2030 will be implemented in order to achieve Kenya’s vision of a high quality life for all Kenyans through energy consumption. With electricity being the most sought after energy service and crucial to the socio-economic development of the country, it is envisaged that by 2015, Kenya will have achieved universal access to electricity with generation capacity at 6.7 GWh/year from 1.86GWh as at October 2013 to slightly over 6,700 MW by 2016. Through this roadmap the generation cost is projected to reduce from US$ 1.130 to $ 0.741, while the indicative end-user tariffs are projected to be reduced from KES 5.89 to KES 3.50 per kWh. The Kenya Electricity Generating Company (KenGen) has installed over 260 MW of solar PV capacity for domestic customers. In achieving this goal, renewable energies will play a critical role as in addition to having the potential to enhance energy security and reduce greenhouse gas emissions, they also have the potential to create employment, generate income, create employment and generate foreign exchange savings. The percentage of renewable energy harnessed is negligible at 0.1% compared with 25% for hydropower and 74% for fossil fuels.

The Kenya Renewable Energy Agency (KERA) estimates that approximately 300,000 rural households in the country have solar home systems installed, and between 10,000 and 20,000 PV systems are currently being sold annually.

# Program Level

There are four crucial aspects for the success of UGEAP with respect to Germany as executing agent for the program:

1. Ability to set up investment vehicles that can execute the Target Investments working through and with local financial institutions.
2. Attract and place capital with private sector investors for UGEAP.
3. Ability to structure the refinancing arrangement with local financial institutions.
4. Ability to structure and underwrite the Target Investments with a first focus on Benin, Tanzania, Kenya, Nigeria and/or Zambia.

Ad 1) Deutsche Bank has a proven track record of set up investment vehicles in various jurisdictions including Luxembourg, Switzerland, Germany, France, Belgium, UK, etc., for the purpose of executing UGEAP. Ad 2) DB continues raising funds for its open-ended funds and as well as for new funds/products it launches. It raises debt and equity in all forms, through multiple sales channels, each with its own merits. The three primary sales channels for funds like UGEAP are:

- Global Client Group (GCG) for large-scale institutional investors like pension funds and insurance companies;
- Structured Sales for medium-sized institutional investors such as foundations;
- Regional Wealth Management advisors (WM) for medium-scale investors such as foundations, churches or family offices.

The cumulative amount of capital raised by these sales units reaches several billions per year through all three sales channels while DB expects that UGEAP will be placed predominantly with investors covered by GCG and SSTD. Together, these units place around EUR 130-150bn of capital with investors. For its Alternatives division, which manages funds similar to UGEAP, Deutsche Asset Management has raised over EUR 82 billion since 2015 (as at March 2016).

Ad 3) DB has already identified partner institutions that are willing and keen to deliver towards the success of UGEAP. DB attaches the memorandum of understandings with both institutions that represent each one of the two options UGEAP shall offer:

- Syndication
- Funding and not participation

Ad 4) DB has the required expertise structuring the Target Investments relying on its infrastructure, corporate finance and trade finance business units which includes the Sustainable Investments team that is in charge to manage concepts like UGEAP. The Sustainable Investments team has a proven track record of executing projects in Africa (including South Africa through existing funds). In total the Sustainable Investments team already covers sustainable assets under management of USD 1.9bn as of now.

### Barriers

As described in section B and D.1 there is a strong need to mobilise debt capital to the benefit of low-emission electricity supply to End Beneficiaries. Public sector funding through development institutions will not suffice to serve the capital by investment needed for the Target Investments. The private sector has a more positive scale towards low-emission electricity supply in the Target Region. Private sector capital is required to be mobilised but:

- The high risk perception of private sector investors.
- The need for long-term debt and equity to meet the high value investments require.
- A local barrier is the high credit intensity of the investments and the lending practices local banks follow that oftentimes focus on collateral based lending for lower margin business or acceptance of a lack of "acceptable" collateral against a higher risk charge (retail business).

### Additionality

#### Close Co-Operation with Local Financial Institutions

UGEAP provides two solutions to local financial institutions to overcome the local barriers. In cases where local banks are also and/or primarily funded by foreign currency funding, UGEAP will not compete with local banks but crowd-in local sources of financing through local banks and co-operate with:

- **long term debt capital**
- **local equity or up regulation / equity capital**

This offers a tailored solution to local banks while details will be tailored to the capacity of local financial institutions. UGEAP is not crowding out, but crowding in local financial institutions. Co-operations with local banks will not be on an exclusive basis. In fact, UGEAP has an interest to sign up as many local partner banks as possible in a single market. UGEAP will also consider other reasons than others than its international credit and quality expectations against the screening criteria which UGEAP will use.

In case energy supply businesses require USD funding, UGEAP will offer an opportunity to local financial institutions to make use of the co-financing of funding together with local bank funding.

### Private Sector Orientation

Investments are private sector oriented, while public sector elements are not excluded (for example: PPA's with national grid operators as an income basis for private independent power producers). There is no competition with public sector initiatives in funding out public sector services. Rather, Target Investments deliver infrastructure where public bodies are unable to provide it.

UGEAP involves private sector investments on its own funding slide, while QGF’s contribution would allow to trigger access to such private sector investment. The QGF will be able to provide a risk buffer against first losses and stable returns and low provision rates are pre-requirements to tap into the large debt market that has a vast amount of funding available for deployment in transactions that produce predictable and stable revenues.

### Concessionality


---
**Page 121**
---

The concessionality of GCF is supposed to provide minimal and consists of tolerance for variable returns. In the base case, GCF’s returns are commensurate with those in the private sector. In addition, GCF is asked to take on the downside first loss risk, which means that GCF can be compensated only if the project fails. Conversely, GCF will be compensated partly for the high downside risk by receiving a larger share of additional returns beyond the target returns for A and B tranche investors. From a risk-return and contribution perspective, this is the least concessionality that was identified in the value stream of capital mobilisation for End Beneficiaries.

The efficiency and effectiveness of GCF are measured against the total project financing and the mitigation/adaptation co-benefits generated by the projects. The following table shows how these compare to an appropriate benchmark. For mitigation, please make a reference to E.6.3 (one indicator for the cost of CO2e).

The efficiency and effectiveness of GCF are measured against:

- The amount of projects that are financed with the support from capital by UEGAP.
- The amount of CO2eq emissions being mitigated in absolute numbers.

As described in section 6.2.1 in combination with section B.3, the capital contribution by GCF and other public investors providing co-financing is expected to be around USD 500 million, which represents about 40% of total capital expenditures of the projects. Forecasts under an expected scenario suggest that GCF’s and other public capital could mobilise 24 times its own contribution given that the repayments from existing investments would not be used to repay capital but rather would be re-invested in new projects. This means that for every USD invested by GCF, it is expected to generate returns at least as high as those expected over a period of 8 years on average. In addition, UEGAP is expected to raise 2/3 of private investment from international investors on top of the planned 1/3 of public investment.

Finally, local financial institutions are expected to contribute their own capital to the projects financed by UEGAP either through direct co-lending mechanism or through straight co-financing in a syndication structure.


### CO2 Savings

DB as investment manager of UEGAP will track the amount of CO2eq emissions being mitigated by the investments financed. DB already operates a proprietary carbon accounting system which has been used in other public-private partnership funds. Based on the estimated level of emission reductions from the 3 transactions that represent the Target Investment Portfolio, the methodology is shown in section E.6.1.

Each investment will be subject to a pre-assessment that will determine the expected lifetime CO2e savings based on the measurement framework applied by the investment manager. Minimum thresholds will apply subject to the technology being used.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 121 OF 165

**<Please specify the expected economic and financial rate of return with and without the Fund's support, based on the private sector IRR (ECA).>**

UGEAP depends heavily on participation of the GCF as B-Capital investor without which it would not come into realization. The financial returns in a base case scenario are as follows:

| Total IRR (Base Case) | 6.7% |
|-----------------------|------|
| Class A IRR (Base Case)| 6.8% |
| Class B IRR (Base Case)| 6.6% |

UGEAP is designed to maximize the available return (expressed by the total IRR) to set the right incentive structure for the private sector investors and the investment manager and allow for the upside to the GCF.

A further analysis on the financial returns is presented in Section F1.

**<Please describe how the Fund's strategy in the long run beyond the Fund Intermediary.>**

UGEAP is a closed end fund concept where the private sector investors have committed to provide within 5 years after the closing of UGEAP, a second and third tranche of capital to be raised over the first 42 months. In case UGEAP's investment activity meets expectations, it is very likely that a follow-on commitment will be made. It will depend on the economic conditions prevailing at the time in question whether funds from GCF or similar entities will be required to mobilize private sector investors again or if the investment climate has improved such that private sector investors would lend commitments without any need for additional funding.

The Fund's strategy is to provide a platform where the private sector can invest in projects that are not financially viable on their own. The Fund will also provide a mechanism through which local financial markets in Africa could have developed to such an extent that local banks can fully satisfy demand for renewable energy projects.

**<Please describe how the GCF's financial strategy in case of private sector operations (e.g., IPOs, public sales, etc.).>**

GCF’s capital invested into UGEAP would have a maturity of 15 years. At maturity, UGEAP is entitled to receive the full principal amount of the investment and any interest that has been earned on it since the date of the initial investment was made using principle that has been collected by UGEAP prior to the maturity date. It is not the intention to replace UGEAP with another investor at this stage to prolong the investment activity of UGEAP. This would typically be done by raising capital for a new fund, which would then invest in projects that are not financially viable on their own.

The asset liability management policy of UGEAP will ensure that private sector investors will have sufficient time to repay GCF’s capital at maturity and that the interest earned over this period may be sufficient to cover any funds being insufficient to repay GCF. In this case, GCF's claim for repayment would be served only partially and in line with the priority of other claims on UGEAP assets.

**E 6.4.4. Use of best practices**

**<Please explain how best available technologies and practices are considered and applied. If applicable, specify the innovations/modifications/adaptations that are made based on industry best practices.>**

At the program level for renewable energy technology, the application of best practice will be ensured through constant interaction with key research centers, other project developers, financiers, energy initiatives such as Sustainable Energy for All (SE4ALL) in order to ensure that best available technologies and sustainable business models are applied. The cost of financing for projects varies from country to country depending on local conditions. Best practices for photovoltaic power plants varies from project to project due to the need to adapt to local conditions certain general features for each project category that were taken into account. have been integrated into the technical solution already in the business development. Given technological development but also changes in behavior of end clients, applied technologies have to be adapted on an ongoing basis.

Best practices in UGEAP's financial arrangements are being adhered to by (i) providing local currency loans to projects, (ii) ensuring that all foreign exchange risk is managed through a dedicated foreign exchange business, so that they can manage the foreign exchange risk on their balance sheet; (iii) ensuring that local banks lend in accordance with international standards and (iv) transparent and frequent reporting to all public and private investors on a regular basis.

Best practices in terms of social, environmental and gender aspects will be covered through the Social and Environmental Management System (SEMS) and/or the Environmental and Social Risk Management Programmes funded by the Green Climate Fund as well as UGEAP's Results Monitoring Framework (see Section H.2).

### Project 1

Amongst solar home systems and rural electrification concepts, the solar home system technology is considered among the best available technology that combines all components necessary to run a technical reliable electricity supply. The technology has been proven in various countries around the world as an effective solution for diversification of household income. The solar home systems provider has also implemented a software database to monitor and manage the installation, maintenance, customer payments, and supervise company operations across the globe. In addition, the provider is training local people in installation and maintenance of the solar home systems, thereby creating sustainable social employment.

### Project 2

The mini-grid provider uses best available practices in terms of its technology and commercial operations in three major ways:

- First, all solar PV systems are sized appropriately for anticipated loads, localized irradiation, and annual seasonality. Best practices in terms of system design, installation, and maintenance are ensured through rigorous quality assurance procedures.
- Second, community outreach and engagement is ensured through capturing local approval for installed projects from the outset. This ensures that the project is well received by the community and has a high likelihood of success. These steps are well established in rural development and are critical for the sustainability of newly introduced technologies.
- Third, the provider works with local PV enterprises to deliver its solution in remote areas. This collaboration with local businesses increases the speed and efficiency at which the provider can deploy electricity connections, while leveraging their knowledge, networks, and trust to preclude feelings of ill will from established enterprises in target communities.

### Project 3

The project developer provides best practice in different aspects:

- A robust and easy-to-handle technology which is reliable, user-tested and technically up to date.
- Hybrid systems are not suitable for use with difficult storage devices (adding batteries would render the solar power system more expensive than diesel-only power), and would need regular maintenance, contain toxic substances, and be environmentally unfriendly.
- In many cases, communities and industries already have diesel generators in operation: The project developer's PV plants integrate existing equipment into an environmental much more friendly and more cost-effective hybrid

### F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

Please provide the narrative and rationale for the detailed economic and financial analysis (including the financial model, taking into consideration the information provided in section C.3.6.3).

The main assumptions of UGAEAPs are based on a simulation of the cash flows using a financial model. UGAEAP as investment vehicle could expect to:

- return from its investments (in form of interest);
- pay back investors in accordance with the structure describe in section C.3 above (in form of coupon payments).

The financial model calculates UGAEAP as an expected investment vehicle distributing the available income and allocating the annual amount of capital to the other stakeholders of the UGAEAP. The results of the financed are dependent on the assumptions made with regards to the investments of UGAEAP. Main assumptions include:

- allocation of the available income;
- allocation of the annual amount of capital that can be invested either through local financial institutions under the FRPA or the syndication structure;
- returns that can be generated on these investments (interest rate, maturity, repayment profiles);
- additional costs associated with the investment (e.g. transaction fees, management fees, etc.);
- external variables that influences the cash flow behavior (currency and interest rate fluctuation);
- expected fluctuations in the value of collateral and principal to the local financial institution / UGAEAP as lender over the lifetime of the loan granted to it; and
- value of collateral at the time the borrower is unable to serve its credit.

Risk factors are generally interrelated. For example, a higher probability of a borrower to default in times of an economic downturn generally also comes with the higher value of available collateral to shrink, increases the potential loss in case the borrower actually defaults and the available collateral is put on sale to recover some of the principal amount. The UGAEAP structure has two main risk factors: (i) the local currency; and (ii) a positive position as two risk factors have a partially offsetting effect with respect to the risks that arise for UGAEAP.

- Risk factor 1: Local currency;
- loss rate.

In terms of foreign exchange (FX) risks, the FX rate used for the first loss calculation would be the lower of (i) the FX rate used at the time the loan was given to the local financial institution and (ii) the FX rate used at the time the same loan was given to the D/E(S)/D or project developers.

In an economic downturn, typically the local currency losses in value while businesses and households face unemployment and reduced income. In such a scenario, the borrower may be unable to service its debt obligations and may be unable to serve their debt payments. If a default occurs, however, the FRP structure uses the current conversion rate of USD to local currency to determine the balance that is to be counted against the first loss position of the local bank in the event of an insolvency. The FRP structure also provides for the full repayment of the principal and employer obligation of the local bank towards UGAEAP under the FRP agreement. While the USD equivalent of loans reduces over time, the local currency value of the loan portfolio increases as a result of the appreciation of the local currency.

The UGAEAP structure provides smaller amounts that would impact UGAEAP as lender to the local bank. This effect cannot be used and is not expected to impact the local bank. In an extreme scenario, local banks may find themselves in insolvency which may be a high risk impact for UGAEAP.


| Table 47: Sensitivity Overview |

### F.2 Technical Evaluation

*Please provide an assessment from the technical perspective. If a particular technological solution has been chosen, describe why it is the most appropriate for this project program.*

UGEAP will only finance proven, bankable technology and sustainable business concepts. All used technologies and technologies under development have been tested by leading international companies within the photovoltaic and battery industry and have been adapted to the local needs for off-grid installations. Supply contracts will foresee industry standard guarantees and warranties. The UGEAP will provide a 10-year performance guarantee, which is extended into an end beneficiary warranty concept to be provided from the project developer to the end beneficiary (Project 1) be transferred to the project (Project 3).

In case of a joint venture, both parties together with DB as investment manager will be a screening of the technologies applied; credit officers will therefore be trained. Additionally UGEAP will work with internationally recognized technical advisors that will ensure proper installation and give a final acceptance to a pre-defined number installations.

Operational concepts follow best practice industry standards. O&M services are done either by the respective project developers that apply international standards (Project 1 and 2) or external O&M contractors (Project 3) that will be well-versed and have a track record in the PV energy market.


## Category 1 & 2 Projects

### Technology

* Solar Home Systems are typically made up of panel, battery, smart controller, light sets and further replenishes, can be easily installed and need no connection to other devices. e.g. no mini-grid. Core element is the proprietary software to monitor and control solar systems, company operations and payments.
* Solar Grid-Connected Mini-grids (SGCM) are typically made up of panel, battery, inverter, low for microgrid; (b) a SHS embedded monitoring system to remotely track usage and payment patterns, battery and panel health. The SGCM can be easily installed and need no connection to other devices. Design improvements, and (c) a PV panel embedded switch to control its power output via mobile network, allowing the system to be turned off remotely in case of non-payment.
* Microgrids are typically made up of panel, battery, smart controller, distribution connections, and individual smart meters for both households and micro-enterprise clients. Other components are photovoltaic panels, batteries, inverters, and a communication link specifically throughout a large village. Distribution cables then connect the production elements its new customers.
* Direct Current (DC) based mini-grids are highly cost effective solution for an easy and efficiently scalable installation of solar systems. The DC system is also more efficient in terms of energy generation and distribution which increases the capital expenditure and the technical requirements.

### Quality management; quality control system; supply chain

Processes have to be in place to control both service and product quality which typically spans from a quality assurance team, customer care and after-sales support. The customer care and assembly staff does the quality testing on a daily basis on the ground.

Designation of a single point of contact (SPC) concept shall be as basic as possible with a focus on an easy customer friendly plug-in installation. While the design the initial engineering of the technical solution is expected to have been performed already prior to the delivery, companies typically partner local solar SMEs for the actual installation.

Training of technicians and sales staff: Frequently self-employed entrepreneurs who are planning to work as service providers will be trained by the company. The training includes a hands-on practical assessment. Necessary training to partner company technicians is provided in order to perform all the tasks required for deployment of the mini-grids also subject to grid working on line voltage (LG lines). For AC grids, certification and training are provided by the supplier.

Suppliers: Suppliers are screened by the investment manager also for quality purposes while generally considered as a part of the supply chain. The company will ensure that all components used in the technology and hardware components are sourced from well known and reputable companies. Components (panels, batteries, inverters, etc.) are selected based on their performance, reliability, durability, long-term operating cost reduction, high quality level, etc. Framework contracts with suppliers are standard. These contracts usually obligate the suppliers to be able to deliver a certain quantity of goods in a given time frame if ordered. In case of any delay or non-delivery, the company will have the right to terminate the contract at any time.

Distributors: Training provided by companies to distributors and agents is reviewed by the investment management team. Distributors are required to be inspected on site by the company on a regular basis with regards to their compliance to the existing rules that are part of the distributor's contracts.

Maintenance: Technical failures are generally dealt with by a local network of maintenance technicians that can be deployed from the central location or from another location where these broken components are fixed and brought back into the spare stock part.

Monitoring: Generally, data regarding system performance and payment is transmitted from the SMS controller to the mobile network and stored in data warehouse systems of the company.


## Category 3 Projects

The main focus of these projects is the combination of solar and diesel to a hybrid solution.

### Technology

- Solar diesel hybrid power generation which integrates a solar farm (to be installed on-site) into existing diesel generator based-on-site / add to existing grid connection.
- Solar PV alone is not cost-effective, meaning that users need either a power storage device (battery) and/or

### F.3. Environmental, Social Assessment, including Gender Considerations

Describe the main outcome of the environment and social impact assessment. Specify the Environmental and Social Management Plan, and how the project/programme will avoid or minimize negative impacts at each stage (e.g., construction, operation, decommissioning). Include information on biodiversity, water resources, air quality, noise, and ESIA standard. Also describe how the gender aspect is considered in accordance with the Fund's Gender Policy and Action Plan.

## Program Level

UGEAP will implement a Social and Environmental Management System (SEMS) at the level of UGEAP as well as at the level of each Investment. The SEMS will be based on GCF’s Social and Environmental Safeguards and strictly follow IFC’s Performance Standards.

## Operational Procedures

Deutsche Bank will classify each project that UGEAP shall invest into according to the categories as set out below:

* **Category A** A proposed investment is classified as Category A if it is likely to have significant adverse social and environmental impacts that are sensitive, severe, or unprecedented. These impacts may affect an area larger than 100 ha.

    * **Category B** A proposed investment is classified as Category B if its potential adverse social and environmental impacts on human populations or environmentally important areas including wetlands, forests, grasslands, etc., are significant but not sensitive, severe, or unprecedented. These impacts may be site-specific; few of any of them are irreversible; and in most cases mitigation measures can be put in place to avoid or minimize the adverse social and environmental impacts.

    * **Category C** A proposed investment is classified as Category C if it is likely to have minimal or no adverse social and environmental impacts.

UGEAP does not expect to consider Category A projects, while through the syndicated loan structure. UGEAP may

As most projects replace diesel-fueled power generation, this will undo the negative effects on air quality associated with diesel generator emissions and will aid in cleaner energy production for the community or site.

All projects will either have an external and independent Environmental Impact Assessment Study, or alternatively in small scale projects where there is no significant environmental impact, a Summary Environmental Assessment (SAE) will be performed. In addition to the following 8 IFC Performance Standards will be performed and impacts in each performance standard will be highlighted and reported on.

- **Performance Standard 1:** Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

- **Performance Standard 5:** Land Acquisition and Relocation
- **Performance Standard 6:** Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources
- **Performance Standard 7:** Indigenous Peoples
- **Performance Standard 8:** Cultural Heritage

Gender is considered a cross-cutting topic that will be addressed across multiple Performance Standards. A separate Gender Policy has been developed as part of DBF programs with the GCF to which UGEAP will need to comply (the "DB-GCF Gender Policy"). The policy is attached as annexes 3 Item 1 – DB-GCF Gender Policy. Within its national context, Deutsche Bank will develop a gender action plan and ensure that Deutsche Bank as executing agency will develop a mitigation and action plan to manage impacts of the sub-projects.

Deutsche Bank will also produce annual reports of the Program's compliance and incidents throughout the development period. The social and environmental impact report will be produced annually by Deutsche Bank from the project developers and the social and environmental report will be produced annually by Deutsche Bank.

Additionally Deutsche Bank will assess and report on wider impacts as outlined in section E. 1: Impact Potential.

# Gender Considerations

The following gender-related impact features have been identified for the company:
- Energy access disproportionately tends to benefit women as they are typically responsible for household tasks.
- In rural areas, women spend more time collecting firewood and water than men.
- Basically, equal chances to create jobs both for men and women.
- Long-term, women will be able to earn income from solar energy.
- The company itself is an equal opportunity employer with a recruitment policy that provides equal chances for both men and women.
- To date approximately one third of the company's workforce is female; there is still a lack of qualified female technicians.

The project targets the roots of gender inequality by:
1) Improving the livelihoods of women and girls through access to clean and efficient home energy sources: empowering women with solar-powered appliances.
2) By improving learning conditions: SHS affect the education level of private households by extending reading hours, particularly enabling girls involved in daytime household chores to do their school homework.
3) By creating new opportunities for women entrepreneurs: The company will provide women with the ability to afford a PV system – the company's pricing and monthly payment plan makes energy more affordable for women who are often responsible for managing family finances.
4) The company will also track new entrepreneurial activity by women as a result of their access to electricity.
5) Positive impact on health especially for women and children who spend more time in the house: substituting kerosene/bill by SHS minimizes indoor pollutant such as carbon monoxide, nitrogen dioxide and formaldehyde.

### Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

1. Waste generated only during the construction phase or during system maintenance. Waste is collected and transported to landfill sites.
2. The company has a waste management facility where they are sorted and stored. Once volumes accumulate, the company will contract with an external supplier for refurbishment and recycling.

### Labor and working conditions

Health issues can potentially arise during the construction phase, the operation of the mini-grid, and maintenance activities, including having standard operation procedures and engineering controls (e.g., access control).

### Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

Due to the limited space required for one panel and the company's commitment to using solar energy, there is no significant impact on resource efficiency and causes pollution prevention. There is a minor risk during digging of footings foundations and laying down concrete for the foundation.

### Community health, safety and security

The company has a direct positive impact on local health services if it can be shown that the mini-grid provides electricity to households connected to the mini-grid, and benefits from uninterrupted power supply.

### Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

No land acquisition or resettlement takes place as the required space for the project is already in use by other activities.

### Biodiversity conservation and social-ecological management of living resources

Biodiversity is not directly affected as the mini-grid are located within farming settlements.

### F.4. Financial Management and Procurement

**Describe the program/project's financial management and procurement, including financial accounting, disbursement methods, and auditing.**

#### Program Level
UGAEP is an investment vehicle which will appoint Deutsche Bank as Investment Manager. Additional Deutsche Bank will act as Technical Administrator. A list of tasks including financial management and procurement is listed in C.3 above.

The Investment Administration team is a separate team within Deutsche Bank (alternatively such services could be provided by an external service provider) that will operate under a service level agreement thus guaranteeing a four year period of compliance to DB standards in line with DB's policies covering conditions and requirements towards outsourced services.

Tasks belonging to the Investment Administration include:
- financial reporting
- NAV calculation
- custodian services;
- administrative and portfolio management (loan booking, interest rate calculation and monitoring on covenants and cash flow underlying investment)
- reporting based on input from Investment Management team.

#### Disbursement Process
For a new underlying investment the Investment Administration team will verify that the investee complies with all requirements of the UGAPF, UGF, and IFC/CV recommendations. For UGAEFP the Investment Manager will apply Deutsche Bank's A&R and CVY recommendations which follow the CFAIC recommendations.

The Investment Management team will provide the respective documentation from the potential investor to the Investment Administration team. The portfolio manager will also provide any other necessary documents and information as well as any necessary measures to enable the loan from an assets liability management perspective in terms of liquidity and mitigation of arising market risks, e.g. by entering into derivative instruments.

The Investment Manager provides the Investment Administration team with the following documentation: Loan Agreement, Loan Documents, Signing Certificate and Condition Precedent Documents (i.e. articles of incorporation, extract of trade register, etc.). The Investment Administration team will then review these documents and sign the loan agreement to the Investment Administration team and informs about the scheduled disbursement dates.

Upon completion and sign-off of the respective documents and after receiving the signed payment instruction the Investment Manager conducts the payments, the Investment Administration team executes the payment from UGAEFP's account to the investee. Detailed processes on financial management and administrative management tasks will be outlined in the key

### Financial Management

The investment vehicle will apply IFRS standards. An annual audit of the financials will be performed by an independent auditor as determined by the Board of UGCAF in accordance with the Procurement Guidelines. The Investment Manager will provide the auditors with all necessary information. On the recommendation of the Board, UGCAF interim audits on social and economic safeguards or impact shall be performed on an ad-hoc or regular basis.

### Reporting

Within the investment advisory agreement certain reporting processes, routines and recipients will be defined. Reports to be prepared include:

- **Figures:**
  - Quarterly Net Asset Value Report required under IFRS;
  - Quarterly financial statements (non-audited) which includes information on non-performing assets;

- **Asset-related Reports:**
  - Annual report on portfolio development and portfolio performance indicators
  - Quarterly report on portfolio development and portfolio performance indicators
  - Annual report on social and environmental compliance and impact

### Procurement

Services to be procured by UGCAF include but are not limited to:

- Administrative Services
- Consulting Services
- Auditing Services
- Legal Advisory Services at the investment vehicle level
- UGCAF at the Investment level
- Board Secretary Services
- Other e.g. advisory services on tax or accounting matters

UGCAF in its investment vehicles will have Procurement Guidelines that provide information on the requirements for the assignment of service providers and consultants. These Guidelines lay down the conditions to ensure transparent competition, as well as equal opportunities to all participating service providers and consultants. Further details are given in each request for proposals.

### Types of Procurement Procedures

Ancillary services can be procured by UGCAF in four different ways, depending on the size of the transaction:

1. Tendering: Project/services are awarded through a tendering process allowing candidates invited by the Investment Manager and/or the Board to submit a proposal choosing service providers and consultants from a short list of experts.

**Page 143**

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 141 OF 165

short list is typically prepared through a pre-qualification exercise.

### Selective Tendering:
The Investment Manager is allowed to consult candidates of his choice and negotiates the terms of the contract with one or more candidates. The selection of candidates can be directly established by the Investment Manager based on prior contact with him to five service provider consultants, depending on the project/services budget as specified below.

### Single Sourcing:
The Investment Manager is allowed to consult one candidate of his choice to negotiate the terms of the contract.

### Direct Implementation:
The Investment Manager carries the activities out directly, where needed complemented by additional expertise coming from external service providers and consultants. Such activities primarily include those that are related to due diligence and project preparation for the investment vehicle.

## Selection of applicable Procurement Procedures

The Investment Manager shall give preference to the Tendering or Selective Tendering processes; whereby:

- **Tendering should be used** for project/services above USD 280,000 (excl. value-added tax). For the avoidance of doubt, Selective Tendering will be applied, if it can be reliably estimated at the time of project/services procurement that the total cost of the tendered services or goods will not exceed the threshold of USD50,000. Selective Tendering shall be the preferred procurement procedure in case of selecting one service provider and Direct Implementation for project/services below USD 50,000. In cases where the total cost is less than USD 50,000, the use of the Board Secretary will not be subject to Selective Tendering due to the sensitive nature of documents such Board Secretary will be required to review including but not limited to private sector investment vehicle documentation.

### Single Sourcing or Direct Implementation should be used for project/services below USD 50,000. Direct implementation is allowed in any case where:

- **Research & Analysis**, whenever such project/services are close to the core project/services of the Investment vehicle or inform the Investment vehicle in defining its strategic direction.
- **One-off services** that do not have a significant impact on the objectives of the Investment vehicle as approved from time to time by the Board of the Investment Committee.

Tenders and the decisions on procurements will be overseen by a procurement committee. Tenders will be oriented towards ICBs and NCIBs to the degree possible and feasible.

## Evaluation and Selection

The preparation of the tender documents, the conductation of the entire tender process and procedure as well as the selection of service providers is carried out by the Investment Manager. The evaluation of service providers’ and consultants’ expertise and proposals is based on a series of factors. In case of the Tendering, these shall be specified, with their respective weights in the request for proposal issued to possible respondents. Indicative factors include the technical capacity, experience, reputation, financial stability, and the terms of engagement. The methodology proposed for the services, qualifications and experience of key personnel included to render the

If changes in the scope of services are introduced by the UGEAP Board for USD 20,000, the Investment Manager may accept these changes. Evaluation reports can be made available to the UGEAP Board upon request.

Monitoring, Reporting and Terms & Conditions, Review Periodicity

The Investment Manager shall review conditions of each UGEAP service provider and shall procure competitive bids using the procurement procedures set out above when considered necessary by the Board. Based on the approval of the Board the Investment Manager is responsible for supervising and managing the service provider's day-to-day operations. ## H.1 Logic Framework.

*Please specify the logic framework in accordance with the GCF's Performance Measurement Framework under the Results Management Framework.*

### H.1.1 Paradigm Shift Objectives and Indicators at the Fund Level

#### Paradigm shift objectives

At the program level, UGEAF supports the paradigm shift to a low-emission sustainable development pathway by:

- Enabling local financial institutions to grow their financing activities of businesses in the clean energy sector through the supply of long-term debt capital and technical assistance.
- Tapping into the deep pool of international capital supply and triggering access to private capital at scale for clean energy supply.
- Reaching local end beneficiaries (households and VSEMs), thereby facilitating impact at a larger scale and within shorter timelines.
- Doubling the amount of GCF's capital which will be leveraged by a factor of 4, thereby providing a show-case for blended public-private finance in SSA.

#### Shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways

As the level of single transactions and projects, the shift of the paradigm to a low-emission sustainable development pathway is supported by:

- Combining sustainable economic growth and climate change mitigation by financing clean energy supply.
- Introduction and innovation by financing state-of-the-art proven technology and thereby support the rapidly growing industry to gain scale.
- Scalability of the business models and the related technologies due to high standardization and proven business cases in the sector.
- Bundling financial service with technical solutions which overcomes the barrier of high up-front costs for households and businesses that in the past favored fossil fuel-based solutions due to their significantly lower or no up-front investment needs; and
- Availability of local currency debt finance for projects generating local currency revenues.

**Footnote:** Information on the Fund's expected results and indicators can be found in its Performance Measurement Frameworks available at the following link (Please note that some indicators are under refinement): [http://www.africafund.org/fileadmin/africafund/documents/Operations.03_Initial_EMF.pdf](http://www.africafund.org/fileadmin/africafund/documents/Operations.03_Initial_EMF.pdf)

## H2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

**Please specify institutional setting and implementation arrangements for monitoring and reporting. Please indicate how you will organize mid-term and final evaluations.**

# Information and Reporting Framework

Within the investment advisory agreement certain reporting processes, routines and recipients will be defined. Communication of information to investors will take place via Deutsche Bank as custodian or directly by the Investment Manager. The website will act as information exchange platform targeting investors as well as stakeholders. In parallel to the information that will be made public, reports will also be distributed to specific recipient groups.

Overall, key reports to be prepared include:

- Quarterly:
  - To be published online: o Key fact sheet outlining the Fund's activities in the reporting quarter, including information on new investments, portfolio performance, climate impact indicators such as CO2 savings; o Earnings report for each investor with a summary per investor;
  - For investors only through the custodian: o Quarterly audited financial statements prepared according to requirements under IFRS; o Quarterly, non-audited valuation report detailing the net asset value of each investor's share at quarter's end;

- Annual Report:
  - To be published online: o An annual report on the operations of the UGEAP covering the following sections: Activities related on investments; Activities related on funding; Social and environmental compliance report including impact reporting;
  - For investors only through the custodian: o Audited financial statements in accordance with IFRS;
  - Annual investor reports: o Audited financial statements reflecting net asset value of each investor's share at the end of the financial year.

Quarterly and annual investor reports will include data on the leverage GCF would have achieved with its investment portfolio.

An impact evaluation can be conducted at the end of the investment term.


# Institutional Setting and Implementation Arrangement for Carbon Reporting

In its function as investment manager of the UGEAP, Deutsche Bank will further assume the obligation to:
- assess the expected CO2 savings from a single investment prior to the investment (forward-looking assessment);
- monitor the actual CO2 emissions of the UGEAP that also define eligible investments from a carbon emission reduction perspective;
- monitor/estimate the actual CO2e savings as a result of UGEAP's investment over the lifetime of the

## Analysis & Monitoring Process

Within the investment guidelines and the operating manual monitoring, reporting and verification processes, routines and responsibilities will be defined. The overall process can be divided into 4 steps covering the lifetime of an investment:

- Pre-Assessment of the targeted investment
- Due diligence on expected CO2/ energy savings (annual and lifetime)
- Monitoring of the realized CO2/ energy savings (annual and lifetime)
- Verification of the reported CO2/ energy savings (annual and lifetime)

The following chart depicts the relevant actions in the different phases and the results:

| Phase | Pre-Assessment | Due Diligence | Monitoring | Verification |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Action** | Check if transactions fit into the project profile of CO2/ energy savings or other impacts | Calculation of expected CO2/ energy savings using methodologies for projects outlined in section 1.2 | Collection of reports from project developer | Calculation of CO2 numbers using agreed methodologies |
| **Outcomes** | Eligibility of transaction | Expected CO2/ energy savings (annual and lifetime) | Number of installations installed with funding | Realized CO2/ energy savings |

Source: Deutsche Bank

The following process chart provides a detailed overview of the different steps and interaction between the different parties involved in the investment as well as the relevant interaction with the owner of the installation that shall receive funding facilitated by UGEAP.

Graph 26: Process chart on information flow and responsibilities

## Source: Deutsche Bank

Selected investments opportunities that fall into the target range of the UGSEAP have been analyzed by the carbon team using the eligible ones with the most appropriate methodology, the results of which are presented in section E.1.2. While on selected installations data from operations were available, DB has chosen to apply a unique approach for all projects and use the same methodology as that used in the UGSEAP. This is done in order to provide for a consistent approach.

However DB's carbon accounting procedures and guidelines cover the whole spectrum of potential scenarios for an investment opportunity, including methodologies that are approved by the CDM. This is to increase the quality of information and address the limitations of assumption based approaches, i.e. CDM methodology used when there was no actual data available. The UGSEAP has also included a number of methodologies that also deviate from the kind of installations that formed the basis when the methodologies were developed.

Additional to the methodology outlined in Section E.1.2., the standards used by Deutsche Bank to calculate and report results are as follows:

- **Key calculation principle:** Energy savings (when actual data available)
- **International Performance Measurement and Verification Protocol (IPMVP):** IPMVP 3rd Edition
- **IPMVP Concepts and Practices for Determining Energy Savings in Renewable Energy Technologies Applications (2003).**

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 10 OF 165

### IPMVP Concepts and Practices for Determining Energy Savings in New Construction (2006)

**Key calculation principle:** Measured savings and verification standards

- **Focus calculation:** Measurement and verification standards
    - EU Directive 2006/32/EC
- **Key calculation principle:** Carbon savings and accounting
    - ISO 14067:2009

### Static data sources:
- GHG Protocol Stationary Combustion Tool (2014)
- DEUTA (2015)

DB foresees that these standards would also be applicable to UGEAP since the existing CO2e savings reporting infrastructure shall be used of UGEAP also.

For each investment, the baseline and savings calculations will be aligned with the most appropriate international reporting protocols. These decisions will be influenced by the type of technology, available data and size of investment.


## Institutional arrangements for monitoring socio-economic benefits

Through its existing investment funds DB has experience in requesting reporting from investees on socio-economic impacts. The investee is required to provide a report containing relevant information and supporting documentation and investors are asked to report on them in regular intervals. In some cases, DB collaborates with experienced parties as compliance advisors. UGEAP can follow a similar model, whereby regular reporting will be requested of the investees. It may also be possible that an independent third party (such as the International Labor Organization or a local university or research institute) can verify and triangulate the data provided.

Cotonou, January 22, 2016

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the UGEAP programme in Benin as included in the funding proposal submitted by Deutsche Bank AG to us on 20 January 2016.

The undersigned is the designated representative of the Directorate General of Climate Change Management, the National Designated Authority of Benin. Pursuant to GCF decision B.09/10, the context of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the UGEAP as included in the funding proposal.

By communication dated 23 January 2016, we confirm that:

(a) The government of Benin has no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Benin's national priorities, strategies and plans.
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal complies with the applicable international standards.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed. We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Yours sincerely,

Dear [Name],

Re: Funding proposal to the Green Climate Fund by Acumen's – "KawSafi Ventures Fund" and Deutsche Bank's – "Universal Green Energy Access Platform"

We refer to Acumen's KawSafi Ventures Fund and Deutsche Bank's Universal Green Energy Access Platform programs to be implemented in Kenya as included in the funding proposal. In line with the UNFCCC, our Nationally Determined Contribution submitted to the UNFCCC, Kenya aims to achieve a low carbon climate resilient development pathway by 2030. The proposed projects will contribute towards achieving 143MCO2eq in line with our sustainable development agenda. This will include, among others, the promotion and implementation of expansion in geothermal, solar and wind energy renewable energy sources, renewable use of biofuels, waste, the need for international support in the form of finance, investment, technology development and transfer, capacity building.

ISO 9001:2008
